TIME’s AI Agents Do The Coordinating, And Its Sellers Still Do The Selling

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

If an AI agent can turn an advertiser’s RFP into a proposal in minutes, what’s left for a publisher’s ad sales team to do?

A lot more selling, said Mark Howard, TIME’s chief operating officer.

In the recent past, when an RFP would come in from a potential buyer, it would kick off a flurry of activity – and a series of labor-intensive tasks – that had little to do with actual selling. Once a rep logged the RFP details in Salesforce, the hand-offs would begin.

Alerts would fire off via Slack, product experts would be dragged into Google Meets to map out inventory across a large product suite and notes would get blasted over email. Meanwhile, the sales team would spend days manually coordinating across various platforms to stitch together a response.

“Premium media sales is still a highly personal, relationship-driven business,” Howard said, “and sellers lose too much time playing coordinator.”

To help ease that operational bottleneck, TIME has been working with Kana, a startup founded by Tom Chavez and Vivek Vaidya, serial ad tech entrepreneurs and the dudes who co-founded Krux, the DMP Salesforce bought roughly a decade ago.

In 2019, the pair launched super{set}, a nontraditional VC firm that co-founds, builds and incubates data and AI startups.

Along with Kana, its portfolio includes privacy platform Ketch; data clean room provider Habu, which LiveRamp bought for $200 million in 2024; and Rembrand, an AI-powered virtual product placement company that agreed to merge with content ad platform Whisper World just last week.

Kana, which came out of stealth in February with a $15 million seed funding round, builds AI agents that take over the grunt work of the ad business.

TIME is using two of Kana’s agents. One works alongside the sales team, reading RFPs and drafting proposals. The other works with ad ops, watching live campaigns and recommending what to do when one starts to fall behind.

The sales agent draws on what Vaidya refers to as a “knowledge graph,” a shared pool of context Kana builds by pulling from TIME’s Salesforce CRM, order management system and ad server, both direct and programmatic.

It also brings in data from live campaigns, which is atypical for a pre-sales tool, Vaidya said. Once a deal closes, this type of data is usually the domain of ad ops, which uses it to look back at how a campaign performed, not to plan the next overture.

The sales agent can proactively turn that information into raw material for pitches.

“We do the research, we look at historical data and we look at similar advertisers,” Vaidya said. “Then a salesperson can go in and start ideating on new proposals for advertisers that TIME hasn’t even sold to yet.”

But the relationship itself still belongs to the salesperson, Howard said, and TIME isn’t looking to automate that or make it into an agentic process.

“We don’t do it with our journalism,” he said, “and we’re not going to do it with our sales.”

Well, at least, “that isn’t the plan for now,” he added.

Once a deal closes, the work shifts to ad ops, and to Kana’s second agent.

Ad ops teams already have dashboards that show them when a campaign is underdelivering or not pacing properly. What those dashboards don’t say, though, is what to do about it, Vaidya said.

The Kana agent answers that for each campaign and recommends a fix, like changing trafficking, pacing or budget, so a shortfall doesn’t turn into a make-good.

TIME’s revenue operations team, meanwhile, is also using the tool, Howard said, and he’s seen “a significant increase in what they’re able to accomplish in any given day or week.” It’s also helping with budget planning.

“And not just for how we’re going to finish this year and how Q1 looks, but actual 2027 modeling,” Howard said. “It’s a whole new world when you can have these kinds of insights delivered to you.”

Between proposals and pacing, agents now cover most of a deal’s life cycle, so there’s technically nothing stopping a publisher’s agent from dealing directly with an advertiser’s agent while the sales rep heads out for a three-martini lunch.

Kana could build that, Vaidya said. Put an MCP server (a standard way for AI agents to connect to other software) in front of the proposal agent, and it would be able to talk to an advertiser’s agents tomorrow.

“That’s a technology problem, which is easy to solve,” he said.

Selling is still built on relationships, Howard said, and agents doing the deal is “just not in our game plan.”

Why the midterms may not spell doom for ad tech consolidation. Plus, how sports leagues are putting ad revenue before player safety.

Agentic media buying is the No. 1 topic du jour in ad tech discussion circles. But how does agentic buying even work when it comes to buying TV and streaming ads? And how does that workflow differ from programmatic? The startup Swivel aims to address those questions.

AppLovin is growing at a blistering pace, but Wall Street investors remain cautious about the business.

High-end ad blockers mimic some of the same behavior as bot traffic. So WiseGuyGaming repurposed the tools it was already using to identify bots to reveal its ad-blocking audience as well.

Criteo has weathered considerable storms in its day, including a painful moment in March 2020 when its market cap bottomed out below $500 million. Can it pull off yet another turnaround?

Disaffection and outright anger toward US sports institutions run deeper than logo-cluttered fields. Leagues and teams are sacrificing player safety and fan enjoyment for marketing dollars.

AdExchanger is where marketers, agencies, publishers and tech companies go for the latest information on the trends that are transforming digital media and marketing, from data, privacy, identity and AI to commerce, CTV, measurement and mobile.

Why altcoin season faces a reality check after $800B market cap plunge

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

The altcoin market cap has dropped from $1.85 trillion to $1.05 trillion at press time in just a month. At the same time, the Altcoin Season Index was trading at 44, indicating that the market was still in a Bitcoin [BTC] season.

The chart below clearly shows that the index has repeatedly attempted to move higher but has struggled to sustain readings above 75.

As expected, this has prompted a question: Is altcoin season now off the radar in 2026?

Alphractal analysis shows that around August to October 2026, both Bitcoin’s 60-day return and the median altcoin’s return climbed to roughly +50-60%, with altcoins moving in sync. But, by November, both lines have dropped back to roughly 0%, and the median altcoin sits slightly below Bitcoin.

Simultaneously, the median altcoin vs. Bitcoin chart shows the gap directly, as it was standing at -5.91%. The green spike in October (about +25%) has fully reversed into red, meaning the median altcoin now trails Bitcoin by about 6% in over sixty days.

This shows how the pattern fits earlier cycles, where altcoin outperformance came in short bursts that faded quickly. Currently, altcoins are weaker than Bitcoin but not in deep capitulation.

Remarking on the same, João Wedson, founder and CEO of Alphractal, argued,

Altcoins signal Bitcoin tops with a high degree of probability!

Echoing similar sentiments, Benjamin Cowen, founder & CEO, ITC, added that “Q4 has arrived,” but the price chart he attached suggests a broad red day, with Bitcoin at about $74,875 (-1.19%), but altcoins are falling harder.

Adding more to the ongoing concern, Wealthmanager also called the current market situation a “full capitulation” phase with about $380 billion more selling than buying, claiming it is seven times worse than 2022’s bottom.

At the same time, Bitcoin dominance is stable above 60% at press time, and altcoins not gaining share fits the weak Altcoin Season Index very well.

However, ETF flows on 6th November show net outflows almost everywhere. Starting with BTC ETFs, which lost $1.28 billion, and Ethereum [ETH] ETFs, which recorded $365.28 million, the second-largest outflow.

Lastly, Hyperliquid [HYPE] was hit by $15.62 million in outflows, Solana [SOL] recorded $26.25 million, Avalanche [AVAX] recorded $1.27 million, Chainlink [LINK] saw $1.06 million, and XRP, Litecoin [LTC], and Binance [BNB] each recorded outflows under $1 million.

It was only Hedera [HBAR] that recorded an inflow of $3.01 million. In other words, institutional money is leaving both Bitcoin and altcoins, not rotating from one to the other. This followed a recent report by AMBCrypto, which already indicated at the end of October that altcoin speculation has slowed down.

CHAPEA Mission 2 Crew Reflects After 378 Days on Simulated Mars

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

After 378 days without windows, the sounds of the outside world, or real-time calls with family and friends, four research volunteers stepped out of NASA’s Mars Dune Alpha habitat on Oct. 31, completing the agency’s second yearlong Mars surface simulation.

Ross Elder, Ellen Ellis, James Spicer, and Matthew Montgomery entered the roughly 1,700-square-foot, 3D-printed habitat at NASA’s Johnson Space Center in Houston on Oct. 19, 2025, for CHAPEA (Crew Health and Performance Exploration Analog) Mission 2.

The crew participated in simulated Marswalks, robotic operations, habitat maintenance, exercise, and crop cultivation.

“Their work means more than completing a mission,” said Johnson Director Vanessa Wyche. “It is about preparing for the challenges of human exploration beyond Earth, gathering the knowledge we need to protect crew health and performance, and paving the way for future missions to the Moon and, one day, Mars.”

NASA astronaut Jack Hathaway put the crew’s time in isolation into perspective: While they were inside the habitat, he completed a 237-day mission aboard the space station and returned to Earth.

During that same period, the Artemis II astronauts traveled around the Moon and set the record for the farthest humans have traveled from Earth. NASA also celebrated 25 years of continuous human presence aboard the International Space Station on Nov. 2, 2025.

“The reality of what you guys did here required immense physiological and psychological strength, teamwork, and human grit,” Hathaway said.

Supported by researchers and ground teams across the agency, the CHAPEA crew collected health and performance data while navigating limited resources and communication delays of up to 22 minutes each way.

Elder brought a U.S. Air Force experimental test pilot’s approach to leading the crew. He found simulated Marswalks and robotic exploration “uncannily similar” to operating as part of a flight. From inside the habitat, crew members remotely operated a drone and a rover to survey terrain and identify or collect rock samples.

The demanding Marswalk schedule also sharpened his understanding of the physical preparation future explorers will need. Preparing suits, completing airlock procedures, and making long traverses took time and stamina. He emphasized nutrition, exercise, and rest as essential to sustaining that pace.

His steadfast leadership and mission focus drew praise at egress for keeping the team motivated and improving. For Elder, seeing his crewmates combine their strengths was a highlight of the mission.

“The real magic has been the privilege and pleasure of seeing everyone come together, with the makings of one of the finest teams I’ve ever been a part of,” Elder said.

Ellis, a U.S. Space Force officer with experience at the National Reconnaissance Office, brought a background in military operations and systems engineering. As medical officer, she helped organize biological sample collections and prepare supplies for the crew’s health research.

As procedures became familiar, she emphasized the discipline needed to follow every step carefully. She also enjoyed solving problems with the tools available. “There is a kind of tactical creativity that I find energizing,” she said.

Contributing to human spaceflight research helped sustain her motivation. So did the relationships with her crewmates. “It would be difficult to convince someone who has never lived in isolation that most days, I don’t feel isolated. I have three teammates that I rely on every day to get through the mission.”

She also credited exercise, behavioral health, nutrition, and medical specialists for their coordinated support throughout the mission.

“When I look back on the mission, the most salient point I’ve learned is that a future Mars crew requires a highly integrated ground team,” Ellis said.

Spicer brought experience in satellite communications and spacecraft development to the habitat’s maintenance work. At egress, he was thanked for keeping its systems running smoothly and described as the “flight engineer extraordinaire and resident data scientist.”

He compared the year to living aboard a submarine or boat, where limited supplies and space shaped daily decisions. The crew tracked consumables from dish soap to respirator filters and carefully managed food and water.

The crew had access to resources that provided news, and Spicer was surprised by how little he missed constant news and social media updates. The break from distractions helped him focus on the mission.

Inside the habitat, stories and jokes around the kitchen table brought laughter to demanding days. He told his crewmates they had made the year “not a chore, but a joy.”

“We’re inspired every day knowing that the data we’re generating is informing Martian mission planning and increasing the safety, health, performance, and comfort of future Martian crews,” he said.

Montgomery brought experience in hardware engineering, robotics, technology startups, and independent films. At egress, he was described as the “Swiss Army knife of crewmates” for his technical knowledge and problem-solving skills.

He helped crewmates identify rock samples collected during simulated Marswalks and set up the habitat’s 3D printing capabilities. His background in indoor agriculture also carried into a seed-germination experiment that used a control and simulated Martian soil.

Maintaining a consistent data set remained a priority. Montgomery learned to investigate anything that gave him pause, describing instances when speaking up helped identify concerns with equipment, safety, and data quality.

He also learned to give himself more grace when an outcome fell short.

At egress, he credited the crew’s different perspectives with producing solutions stronger than any member could have developed alone.

“It’s really the people that make exploration possible,” he said.

Two weeks of post-mission evaluations and debriefs will help researchers interpret the crew’s data alongside their experiences. The study builds on the first CHAPEA mission, which concluded July 6, 2024.

Grace Douglas, CHAPEA’s principal investigator, said the research will help NASA weigh human health and performance risks against resources, including crew time, communication capacity, and food and water system designs. She also noted that many resource challenges facing space explorers mirror problems on Earth.

Sara Whiting, CHAPEA project scientist, said the data comes as NASA plans how astronauts will live and work at the Moon Base, humanity’s first lunar outpost near the Moon’s South Pole.

“This is really the now moment for this kind of data,” she said.

NASA explores the unknown in air and space, innovates for the benefit of humanity, and inspires the world through discovery.

Studio Aslak Siimes designs PharmaPrinter Aurum to "empower pharmacists"

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Industrial design firm Studio Aslak Siimes and Finnish startup CurifyLabs have created a pharmaceutical compounding system, designed to improve the process of creating patient-specific medicine.

The PharmaPrinter Aurum was born from the realisation that almost 30 per cent – nearly a third – of all medication requires personalisation rather than being mass-manufactured, according to studio founder Aslak Siimes.

This compounding, when you combine different ingredients to make tailored medication, is usually done by pharmacists using traditional methods such as a pestle and mortar.

CurifyLabs and Studio Aslak Siimes wanted to create a machine to automate part of this process and enable pharmacists to achieve “system-guaranteed” dosage consistency while eliminating friction in the process.

“To put it simply, the main concept behind Aurum was to reduce the possibility of human error from the compounding workflow and empower the pharmacists to bring high-quality compounded medication to their patients with ease,” Siimes told Dezeen.

The Aurum design is part of a wider ecosystem of products from CurifyLabs, a health tech company that Studio Aslak Siimes has worked together with for two years.

The machine comes in after a doctor’s prescription arrives and a compounding pharmacist picks the accurate recipe for the prescription, known as a blueprint.

“[The] active pharmaceutical ingredient (API) and excipient base are carefully weighed, mixed and put into a syringe, which is then installed to Aurum’s heating jacket and inserted into the machine,” Siimes said.

“We have a patented syringe plunger attachment called gripper, where you simply squeeze a lever and move the gripper against the syringe plunger manually and then release it. It attaches tightly and works as the movement to control the dispensing from the syringe.”

Blisters for the medication, which are also designed by the studio, are then placed on top of the scale in a dedicated blister holder.

“The scale handles weight-based quality control,” Siimes explained. “Then you start the print, and it dispenses fully accurate compounded medication in exact dosage quantity and weight.”

“The print is fast, and a few moments later the ready blisters are removed, sealed and labelled with dosage-specific labelling, and then it is ready for distribution to the patients,” he continued.

“I guess in short: put medication into syringe, put syringe into Aurum, press play and you have ready meds.”

The machine also has interchangeable modules to let pharmacists create different dosage forms, including tablets, films, suppositories and troches within one system.

Siimes believes that his design is the “only fully working semi-automated compounding ecosystem in the world”, and the product is already on the market and used by compounding pharmacies and hospitals around Europe and North America.

“The technology is already present in 30 states in the USA, and the reception has been highly positive,” Siimes said.

“From the client base, we hear that we are truly making a difference, and can help them to deliver better products to patients with a system that really works and is easy to operate,” he continued.

“It has been such a lovely thing to notice that all the hard work put into it really translates into positive outcomes.”

Other healthcare products recently covered on Dezeen include a wearable patch that detects heat exposure and a meter for at-home hormone level readings.

The housing laws that could derail a sale — and why Realtors need to pay attention

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

At NAR NXT 2026, industry speakers said DSA-aligned housing policies are influencing local tenant laws. They highlighted tenant right of first refusal, just cause eviction rules, and source of income protections as issues affecting rentals, investors, and some mortgage underwriting.  AI Summary

This past week’s midterm elections saw over 100 candidates endorsed by the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA) win their respective races at the federal, state, county and municipal level. While the National Association of Realtors (NAR) does not solely support one political party, instead endorsing candidates that support housing policy positions that align with NAR’s goals, Realtors are raising the alarm when it comes to some of the policies being enacted by elected DSA lawmakers.

“I am Chicken Little and I’m here to tell you that the sky is falling. But it is our responsibility to lift it up and make sure we’re aware of what’s going on so that we’re prepared,” Stacey Johnson-Cosby, an agent at ReeceNichols, a RealTrends Verified firm, said at a session during NAR’s 2026 NXT conference on Sunday. “In 40 years in the business, I’m seeing stuff now — laws and regulations that I would never have imagined and to this day can’t imagine that they’re in our marketplaces today.”

As Johnson-Cosby noted, the DSA does not believe in private property rights. Instead the group believes that housing is a human right and that properties should be owned by either the government or non-profit organizations.

“Their North Star is true social housing — housing that’s backed by public dollars. They feel housing is a human right and they’re going to make sure there is housing for everyone, whether you can afford it or not,” Johnson-Cosby said.

The things the DSA is advocating for beyond social housing, include universal rent control, tenants right to counsel and tenant organizations to organize things like rent strikes.

“Their goal is to end land and real estate speculation and decommodify housing — housing should not be for sale,” Johnson-Crosby said. “Anybody in here agree with that?”

Ending private property rights in America would not only be a drastic change for the U.S. economy, but it would also be a massive disruption to real estate and housing professionals nationwide, who are in the business of helping consumers buy and sell homes.

“They want to get rid of the concept of the white picket fence. To us, that’s the American dream. And we need the white picket fence for our clients to build wealth,” Johnson-Crosby said. “That’s our business.”

As of right now, things have not progressed this far, but Johnson-Crosby and Monica Gilroy, a founding member and managing partner at Gilroy Law Firm, said that where they are seeing an impact from DSA lawmakers and advocates is in the investor and rentals space.

Over the past decade Gilroy and Johnson-Crosby said they have seen the rise of what have been termed “tenant protection laws,” a term Johnson-Crosby does not love.

“I’m going to push back against the term tenant protection laws if the tenants are our customers. Nobody will protect our customers better than we will, right?” Johnson-Crosby, who is also a property manager, said. “We don’t need laws and elected officials and all of these groups to come in and protect our customers from us. We need them as much as they need us. We’re two sides of the same coin.”

Some of these tenant protection laws include rent control, source of income protections, just cause eviction rules, eviction moratoriums, tenant right to counsel, tenant right of first refusal and tenant bill of rights.

“These are the laws that are coming. If they’re not in your neighborhood or your jurisdiction or your city now, at some point the goal is that they would be,” Johnson-Crosby said, noting that it is important for not only property managers, but also Realtors to be aware of them if they are working with clients looking to buy or sell an investment property. “They’re spreading across the country.”

The laws Johnson-Crosby and Gilroy said agents and property managers should be most concerned about are tenant right of first refusal, just cause eviction rules and source of income protections.

Under a tenant right of first refusal, if a landlord wishes to sell a property, they must first provide the tenant with the option to purchase the home.

“Every real estate agent needs to know this. If your property that you’re listing has a renter in it, in some jurisdictions, you have to check with the renter first and give them the opportunity to determine if they’re going to buy the property,” Johnson-Crosby said. “It could be 30 days. It could be 90 days. It could be six months. It depends where they’re holding up the ability to put that house on the market until they determine if they can buy it and in some cases, they can sell the rights to a nonprofit. If this is in your marketplace, you need to know that so that your clients won’t put a house on the market, skip the tenant and end up losing money.”

With just cause evictions, renters are allowed to stay in a property as long as they want.

“If they’re not violating the law or the lease, you can’t remove them,” Johnson-Crosby said. “They are in your property for as long as they want, in perpetuity.”

If an agent is working with an investor, Johnson-Crosby and Gilroy said they need to be aware of these laws, so their client knows the risks they may be facing in venturing into this business.

While these laws can certainly have an impact, the one Johnson-Crosby and Gilroy said agents need to be especially aware of is source of income protection. In some jurisdictions, source of income has become a protected class under fair housing laws, meaning that a person cannot be denied housing based on their source of income, which may be alimony, gig work, rental assistance programs or a housing choice voucher. The denial of housing due to source of income has become the subject of multiple lawsuits over the past few years tied to rentals, but this is also a concern for lenders as it is pushing into the mortgage space when looking at someone’s ability to obtain a mortgage.

“Fair Housing is obviously something that is crucially important in our industry and we want to always be sure we’re doing everything we can to stay as compliant as possible with it,” Gilroy said.

While many of these laws are currently happening at the local level, Johnson-Crosby and Gilroy said agents need to keep an eye on these things as they may be coming to their market next.

“This is going on throughout the United States, and one of the worst things that can happen — it spreads to other states. If it happens at a national level, you’re going to be impacted,” Johnson-Crosby said. “And it can happen at the White House through regulatory agencies.”

This is why Johnson-Crosby said Realtor members need to be in contact with their local, state and federal lawmakers.

“If [lawmakers] want to create more housing, have us at the table so we can tell you what really works — from the people who actually create the housing,” Johnson-Crosby said. “We met with the state reps and we got a law put into place that pre-empted source of income in our state and we killed it through advocacy.”

The GOP went all in on Trump. It’s a cautionary tale about leadership

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Republicans hitched their wagon to a single leader. And they lost in the midterms. Bigly.

In what turned out to be a historic and unprecedented midterm election, Democrats came out with some major victories. States turned blue that have been led by Republicans for decades, like Kansas, South Carolina, and Texas. And even states that voted President Donald Trump into office just two years ago swung left. 

Democrats, who will take control of the next Congress, are celebrating, as what was expected to be a blue wave turned out to be a blue tsunami. 

Meanwhile, Republicans are struggling to wrap their heads around their new reality, as it seems clear that change is coming. And it could serve as a lesson for what happens when an organization puts all its eggs in one leader’s basket.

What Republicans are saying

Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis called the blue wave an “extinction-level event” for Republicans. Ted Cruz said the election was “a Watergate-level repudiation.” And conservative podcaster Clay Travis declared that the era of Trump is “over.” 

And while Trump was quick to say Republicans lost because he himself “wasn’t on the ballot” and that Americans didn’t like the candidates, the president had endorsed many of Tuesday night’s biggest losers, who had closely aligned themselves with his policies. 

To name a few, Ken Paxton lost the Texas Senate seat to Democrat James Talarico, Michigan Republican Mike Rogers lost to Democrat Abdul El-Sayed, and South Carolina Republican Darline Graham—who was handpicked by the president—lost to her Democratic challenger Annie Andrews.

Clearly, Americans were tired of Trump’s America. But if anything can be learned from Tuesday’s historic election from a leadership perspective, it’s perhaps that when you’re vying for a job, blindly following any one leader—especially a massively unpopular one—might not be the smartest bet. 

Why blind loyalty to one leader is a hard sell

Blind loyalty is problematic for a few reasons. Keynote speaker and Thirty Minute Mentors podcast host Adam Mendler, who’s interviewed more than 500 of America’s top leaders, tells Fast Company that even good leaders need to be challenged. 

“The most dangerous room for a leader is one where everyone agrees. At some point, you have to ask whether people are following your vision, or simply afraid to challenge it,” he says.

Mendler adds that hitching your candidacy to a single leader isn’t just bad politics. It’s a bad look for anyone striving for authority who wants to be respected as an independent individual—be it in public service, business, or any other situation where people rally behind one person. And just because a leader is popular doesn’t necessarily mean teams should blindly go all in.

“What happens when that popularity disappears?” Mendler asks.

Natalee Gibson, cofounder and CEO of Songue PR, who has trained leaders and corporate executives for 25 years, agrees. She tells Fast Company she sees that same mistake being made frequently in organizations, where workers confuse “proximity to power” with “having power themselves.” Many Republicans, she notes, seemed to make that same calculation with Trump.

Instead, she says what actually characterizes strong leaders is the ability to exercise their own judgment. “A leader is someone who can speak truth to power, no matter how uncomfortable. It is someone who can challenge decisions, ask hard questions, and recognize when something isn’t working and call it out,” she says.

How to actually showcase leadership skills

That philosophy might explain why those who set themselves apart from Trump, including some of his harshest critics, ended up being the ones who walked away victorious on Tuesday. 

Because while some candidates toed the line, others didn’t mince words about their disdain for Trump’s policies—which perhaps partly accounted for why some states that have been historically pro-Trump in past elections so dramatically swung the other way.

The person who maybe did so the best was Abdul El-Sayed, who said during a recent interview on comedian Hasan Minhaj’s podcast that Americans “are being actively bullied by the dumbest people in the room.” He also called Trump an “idiot” and criticized JD Vance for blindly following Trump, saying that his idea of leadership is to “suck up to the strongest person in the room.”

In El-Sayed’s relentless critiques, not to mention his starkly different ideas about the direction the country should go, he set himself apart in a state that voted for Trump in the 2024 election. Not only was he a vocal Trump critic, but he also relied on his own vision—not anyone else’s. From talking about getting money out of politics and healthcare to cutting funds for Israel, he showcased his confidence, his judgment, and his ability to lead. All of that may have played a role in his victory.

Tuesday night proved that leaders need to rely on their own abilities, views, and uniqueness—and not just placate whoever’s above them on the org chart.

Minimum wage, the gender wage gap, and more: How Democrats could pressure Trump on workplace issues

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

While Democrats’ legislative agenda for the incoming Congress is still taking shape, early indications suggest workplace and labor issues will have a prominent role.

And while the Senate filibuster and President Donald Trump’s veto power make it unlikely that new worker protections will become law, their inclusion in the Democratic agenda could keep them front and center in the national debate.

Democrats’ opening slate of legislation will likely include familiar proposals to raise the minimum wage, mandate paid sick leave, strengthen collective bargaining rights, and address the gender wage gap, Bloomberg Law reported.

The party also may pursue bills that would make it easier for workers to sue over their retirement plans and close a loophole related to retirement investment advice.

Democrats will oversee executive branch agencies that enforce federal labor and employment laws, including the Department of Labor, the Equal Employment Opportunity Commission (EEOC), and the National Labor Relations Board (NLRB). 

They can be expected to exercise those powers aggressively.

Party members and labor leaders have already raised concerns about the Labor Department’s Occupational Safety and Health Administration, particularly its reduced enforcement capacity and failure to issue a regulation to protect workers from heat, Bloomberg reported.

Other potential targets include the EEOC, which has recently prioritized protecting white men; the NLRB, which has taken an employer-friendly approach to labor relations; and the Labor Department’s Employee Benefits Security Administration, which has considered allowing private assets (like private equity and private credit) into 401(k) plans.

“There’s going to be a lot of oversight,” Alexander MacDonald, cochair of Littler Mendelson P.C.’s Workplace Policy Institute, told Bloomberg.

Democrats’ federal agenda could also influence the party’s priorities at the state level, where it made significant gains on November 3. Labor priorities like minimum wage hikes and paid family leave laws, mostly concentrated in deep blue states like New York and California, could become law in places like Michigan, Pennsylvania, and Wisconsin.

Business advocacy groups are likely to mount opposition.

“We have concerns about all of them,” Amanda Fisher, state director for the National Federation of Independent Business in Michigan, told Bloomberg, adding that small businesses “just don’t have the staff or the resources to comply with these new mandates.”

This election demonstrated something important about decision-making

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Six months ago, the outcome of the 2026 midterm elections would have seemed impossible. Most states and regions were highly polarized politically. So-called purple states were generally a mix of deeply divided areas acting as a counterbalance to one another, rather than a concentration of moderates and independents.

Yet, states made enormous swings. I spent almost three decades living in Texas (and only moved away last year). A state that had not elected a single Democrat to a statewide office since 1994 saw every statewide office on the ballot taken by Democrats. Similar stories occurred across the country, with Democrats winning in states and districts that had not flipped in decades.

There are three things to note about the election. First, registered Democrats turned out in droves compared with registered Republicans. Second, there were vast differences in polling numbers for different races within states that were often not reflected in the returns. Third, it appears that voters switched the party they voted for as well, though this is harder to validate on an individual basis.

What do these trends tell us about human behavior?

Motivation and action

Nationwide, about half of registered voters actually voted. That is a high turnout for a midterm election, but still means that only one out of every two voters cast a ballot. So, what gets people out to vote?

One factor that drives voting is habit. The best predictor of whether you vote in an election is whether you voted in a previous election. When it becomes a habit to vote, then you do it year in and year out in primaries and general elections.

Beyond habit, though, actions are generally driven by a combination of energy and efficacy. That is, someone needs to be energized to engage and needs to believe the act of voting has an impact on the world. 

Energy comes from dissatisfaction. People are energized when there is a gap between where they are and where they would like to be. 2026 saw a high level of turnout in part because many people felt that gap between where their lives are (and where the country is) and where they (and the country) should be. 

This energy only becomes action when people feel that the voting process matters. When people believe the choice of candidate is important and that getting out to vote influences the outcome, they vote. When you’re trying to engage people in any behavior, then, you want to energize them by emphasizing the gap between where they are and where they want to be, and demonstrating that the desired action will help to minimize that gap.

Choice consistency

One surprising finding was that the polls showed more variability than was observed in the actual election. In Texas (which I was following closely), the polls generally showed James Talarico with a significant lead over Ken Paxton, while at the same time they showed incumbent Greg Abbott with a small but consistent lead over Gina Hinojosa. 

In the end, both Talarico and Hinojosa won. This didn’t just reflect an underestimate of the number of people who would vote for Democrats. If it did, you might expect that Talarico would have had a substantially larger margin of victory than Hinojosa. And while Talarico did have a larger margin (55.1% to 43%) than Hinojosa (52.8% to 45.7%), it wasn’t that much different.

Polls tend to focus on individual candidates or at least present questions about candidates in isolation. As a result, they get a snapshot of people’s thoughts about that particular candidate at that moment. In contrast, when people step into the voting booth, they are making a large number of selections all at once. And when they do that, they often simplify the decision-making to reduce the cognitive effort they have to put in.

A straightforward way to reduce mental effort is to vote by category rather than to treat each contest individually. Picking the candidate by party affiliation rather than by his or her own record is one such approach, and (clearly) many people in the 2026 elections did just that—despite the preferences they expressed in polls. Indeed, in Texas, Democrats didn’t just win a couple of statewide races—they won every single statewide race. 

Outer certainty masks inner chaos

One thing that stumped political analysts over the past decade in the age of Donald Trump was that supporters of the president typically had a seemingly unshakable level of loyalty to him. The prevailing wisdom was that Republicans would support the president despite the high gas prices, rising inflation, and the war in Iran. 

In the aftermath of the election, everyone is looking for the reason why support for Republicans shifted. The mistake in this question is looking for the reason. Instead, we should think more about the psychology of belief.

People have lots of psychological mechanisms that protect the consistency of their beliefs. When there is a belief we want to maintain, we discount new information that contradicts that belief. We minimize its importance. We focus on information consistent with what we believe in rather than information that is inconsistent. We also tend to believe things that the people around us believe. As a result, for a long time—even in the face of lots of contradictory evidence—people will appear to have an unwavering consistency.

Eventually, however, if the weight of contradictory evidence gets too great (particularly if the social support for their beliefs also starts to crumble), people may experience a sudden shift to a new stable configuration of beliefs. Indeed, those beliefs may not just waver, but collapse entirely, and suddenly they find themselves supporting the opposite of what they initially held true.

While many still support the president and the Republican Party, a significant number of people have experienced this exact shift, moving from being supporters of the Republicans to being supporters of the Democrats.

But this consistency masks a slow accretion of information under the surface. People don’t display progressively less confidence in their support of one party followed by gradual support of the other. Instead, at some point there is too much counterevidence for them to maintain their prior affiliation, and they switch. That tendency to shift wholesale is another reason why voters ultimately selected candidates by party affiliation when they got in the voting booth.

So what?

It is rare to see a decision made by so many people in which so much data is collected both in the run-up to the choice and about the choice itself. Drawing lessons about human behavior from these situations is highly valuable. While these factors are crucial if you’re in the business of understanding voters, they are equally useful in daily life. You may not be trying to change election outcomes, but you are likely trying to influence the behavior of those at work and at home. It is worth thinking about how you might use these insights to influence the decision-makers in your own world.

Enterprise AI is waiting for its Transformer moment

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

As a professor of innovation, there’s a slide in my deck I use every time I start a session on artificial intelligence: a fragment of the first page from the “Attention Is All You Need” paper, from 2017. I use just a fragment because I don’t want the students to read it… I usually refrain from using a lot of academic papers when I teach MBAs and executives: they tend to be boring, and this one is no exception and is not particularly easy to read. However, it is historic. Written by eight Google researchers, it is the paper that defines the Transformer architecture, which among other things, became the architecture behind GPT and much of the subsequent generative-AI wave. The rest, as I said, is history.

The Transformer was not only a successful model architecture: it was also a huge architectural discontinuity. The 2017 breakthrough was able to completely reorganize computation around self-attention, to eliminate recurrence and to make parallel training much easier. And in doing so, it did not simply enlarge what was already there: it completely changed the architecture. 

In 2017, Google understood the technology, but underestimated the category it could create. It did not ignore the Transformer: it incorporated the architecture into products such as Translate and Search, but it failed to turn its own breakthrough into the product that defined the next era. OpenAI, founded two years before the paper appeared, recognized its broader potential and built GPT, and ultimately ChatGPT, on top of it. Meanwhile, all eight authors eventually left Google, seven to become founders and one to join OpenAI. For present purposes, “Transformer moment” means an organizing principle whose eventual importance greatly exceeds its initial application. 

Making an artificial worker more capable versus making an organization intelligent

I have been insisting on this for a long while now: an individual, human or artificial, can complete tasks, but a company, on top of that, must preserve state, coordinate specialized capabilities, enforce authority, remember outcomes and adapt across time. In that sense, transformers provided the architectural foundation for extraordinary advances in model-level and task-level capabilities. But from there, there is still a significant gap until you can reach enterprise performance.

In McKinsey’s 2026 survey, 80% of respondents reported improved personal productivity, but only 37% reported a positive contribution to enterprise EBIT, with no significant differences from the previous year. This reveals the distance between individual augmentation and organizational results. McKinsey also notes that the high performers are typically those companies that redesign workflows, rather than simply inserting AI into existing ones. 

In practice, the industry’s current starting point seems to be the agent. Anthropic explicitly begins with the “augmented LLM”, a model supplied with retrieval, tools and memory, while Microsoft presents sequential, concurrent, group-chat, handoff and other multi-agent patterns. These are useful techniques, but nearly all begin with an artificial worker and ask how to connect it to other workers. Is coordinating agents the same thing as designing an intelligent organization? To me, at least, it looks completely different. 

This proliferation of patterns is the evidence of an unsettled foundation

The problem is not just variety: what fails is that every implementation, each agentic project, repeatedly reconstructs the same underlying capabilities, from identity, to permissions, state, policy, evidence, recovery and learning, because no common foundation exists. 

If we take a look at Gartner’s forecasts, they predict that more than 40% of agentic projects will be cancelled by the end of 2027 due to cost, unclear value or inadequate risk controls. This supports the idea that model capability alone is insufficient. Gartner also explicitly recommends focusing on enterprise productivity as a whole, rather than individual task augmentation.

What’s the real problem, then? Simply put, that the agent should not be the organizing unit. The center should instead be a persistent, goal-directed organizational system. Models and agents can contribute reasoning or execution, but the company-level system owns the representation of the business, its operational state, objectives, constraints, authority and accumulated experience. 

In order to work properly for an organization, such an architecture should have persistent organizational state; formal objects, relationships and permissible actions; objectives and trade-offs; deterministic authorization outside the model; ephemeral agents and credentials; escalation mechanisms; and an append-only record connecting intent, action and outcome. Recent NIST guidance strongly supports architectural separation between reasoning and policy, deterministic access decisions and immutable evidence. Microsoft’s reference architecture similarly separates the agent runtime, policy layer, tools, systems of record and audit pipeline. 

What should be the test for a genuine architectural breakthrough?

The evidence to prove a breakthrough should therefore not be whether a system outperforms an agent on some benchmark. A genuine breakthrough should make several apparently separate problems — coordination, governance, memory, replaceability, accountability and continuous learning — much simpler to manage, because they are handled by the same underlying design.

Google invented the Transformer and successfully deployed descendants such as BERT, but it failed to capture the defining consumer product moment in the way OpenAI did, perhaps because they were uncertain about the effects of the technology on their business model, like what Clayton Christensen exposed in “The Innovator’s Dilemma”: Large Language Models were much more expensive to run for web searches, provided a complete answer in such a way that most users wouldn’t bother to click on an ad, and they sometimes hallucinated, endangering the confidence in their results that Google had spent decades building.

Later, Alphabet’s hurried Bard response culminated in errors and roughly $100 billion in lost market value in February 2023. As we can see, research leadership does not automatically translate into product or category leadership. Google has improved a lot since then and has outperformed other competitors on several occasions, but at the time, they lost an opportunity to exploit what could have been a clear pioneering advantage. 

When Larry Page and Sergey Brin presented PageRank, they tried to license their technology to several companies, the internet behemoths of the moment. Yahoo! declined even to meet, Excite doubted PageRank’s business value and declined to license it. Those incumbents evaluated an unfamiliar technological advantage as if it were another search technology whose value they doubted, and continued with what they knew worked for them… until it stopped working. Again, the rest is history. 

Will today’s AI leaders recognize organizational intelligence as a new category? Or dismiss it because its first implementations appear narrower than the eventual architecture? By insisting on putting the LLM in the center of everything, they are making the same mistake that these internet behemoths committed with Google, and a similar one, misreading the category, that Google committed with the Transformer. Placing the LLM or the agent at the center risks optimizing the previous paradigm instead of discovering the next one. 

The current agent-centric paradigm leaves a clearly identifiable architectural gap. Whoever supplies the organizing principle for intelligence at organizational scale will have the opportunity to completely reshape the industry. And the missing architecture will not necessarily be built by today’s model leader. 

The only question is whether today’s AI leaders will recognize that moment, or provide another company with the opportunity to define it for them.

The Fine Print: The following comments are owned by whoever posted them. We are not responsible for them in any way.

Mostly hybrids (28%), but also diesel (4%).

The average cost of electricity in the UK is 26p/kwh.

A typical EV can go 6 km per kWh, so that's 4.3p/km.

The average cost of a liter of petrol ("gasoline") is 170p.

The average car in the UK can go 13 km on a liter. 170/13 = 13p/km.

So driving a petrol car costs three times as much as driving an EV.

An EV owner can sign up for cheaper nighttime rates at half the daytime rate, which would make driving a petrol car six times as expensive as driving an EV.

What can I realistically do with local AI on a Mac mini M5 Pro (48GB)?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

I just got a Mac mini with an M5 Pro (base CPU config) and 48GB of unified memory. I want to run as much AI locally as makes sense, but it's hard to separate what actually works day to day from benchmark hype.

Real tokens/sec numbers and "this was a waste of time" stories are especially welcome.

Qwen3.8-27B is by far the best coding model runnable locally. However, it will be difficult to hold a long context window, and forget about running multiple coding sessions in parallel. It's also going to be annoyingly slow for interactive work. It can be useful if you give it chores to do in the background or overnight. It can be useful as a secondary model like advisor role in oh-my-pi.

Unfortunately, other models smaller than 27B are too dumb for coding (in serious projects you'll get more bugs than help from them).

4-bit quants are optimal for small RAM budget. Going lower starts breaking the models and higher quants give you diminishing results.

gemma4-e4b is small and pretty fast, and works fine as a lossy wikipedia and a smarter regex (searching and summarization where accuracy isn't critical), but don't let it edit anything that you want to keep.

Comfy stuff tends to fit easily in 48GB, but it's a bit slow (most workflows are designed for nvidia cards with less VRAM and more raw power).

I've got 48GB M3 Max and 96GB M5 Ultra. The 96GB is still barely usable for real coding, and financially doesn't make sense compared to Deepseek 4.1 Flash that's smarter, 4x faster than anything I can run, and doesn't OOM when I run more than a couple things at once.

However, it will be difficult to hold a long context window

This might improve a bit if a Qwen4 27b is released since Qwen4 will be replacing the dense attention layers with sparse attention layers.

EDIT: Actually no, I forgot QSA still keeps the full kv cache representation around and only compresses for selection purposes, unlike DSV4.1 which uses the compressed version for attention as well. So performance is improved but not memory usage.

I have the same hardware as you and I've been using MLX-LM with Qwen3.6-35B 6bit quant. My setup is a vibe-coded local server that just wires together a bunch of different components with a web UI. It's fairly fast in terms of token output, but noticeably worse than claude at coding. It can handle routine tasks and I've been using it to scan my codebase for bugs. Low-powered models work pretty well for finding bugs if you make them verify the bug is real. I was also using it for some voice assistant stuff (with a separate speech-to-text and text-to-speech setup) and it was pretty good at handling natural language questions and using tools accordingly (turn the lights on, play music, etc.). I got it to a point where the latency was extremely low and it felt really responsive. As for image generation, the version I was able to get running on my home setup was drastically worse than the big company offerings and I didn't really have much use for it, so I abandoned that part.

My local setup: M1 Max 64GB, LM Studio, pi, and OrbStack for isolation. I get about 60 tps from Gemma 4 26B A4B QAT and prefer MLX if available for a small speed increase. I use this setup when I want complete privacy. Or I'll use it to hear my fan spin up and get a feel for what's happening under the hood, details an API would hide.

That I pay my own power bill is satisfying for part but not all of the ethics problem. I look forward to a local setup being good enough for most anybody, so we can in some form give back to the commons what we took.

For now I'm constrained by models being either too slow on this chip to wait for, or too dumb to depend on. I don't just mean they write bugs, I mean sometimes they think their way into a non-converging fugue state I must abort. But they are much smarter per time than two years ago when I first explored this, and the DIY aspect is fun.

For general questions, I'm happy enough with Siri AI. It's fine for a first try and that's often plenty. And local + PCC is usually private enough.

For automated coding tasks whose results I'll depend on to be good, I'd go back to the two major players and get a subscription again. I don't have one today, but I'd get whichever is not on tap at work.

Check out Unbiased Strata which aims to be a more secure version of Strata. It's a custom inference engine based on llama.cpp (?) tuned to let you run Qwen-Flash-Next locally using as little as 12GB VRAM (plus 96GB RAM). It works by predicting which MoE parameters will activate and hot-loading them into VRAM before they are needed.

I have a 12GB Nvidia GPU on my framework laptop and have been running this for a month or two. It's the best local LLM setup I've used by far. I mostly use it with the Pi harness, which took a little tuning to get the context window and certain token limits right. I don't have hard numbers off the top of my head but I've found it to be a little slower than Opus, and still very usable. You can probably get higher speeds on 48GB.

Unattended reboots with encrypted disks

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

November 7, 2026 · About 7 minutes · Tags: blogsystem5, zkeys

This article was originally published on Substack in the Blog System/5 newsletter and is replicated here for archival purposes.

Do you run a small home server or NAS holding your precious photos and sensitive documents? If so, I suppose the drives are encrypted, right? But then… can you reboot the machine without manually entering a passphrase before being able to access your files again?

If you answered yes to the questions above—which is common for convenience—that implies the encryption keys live in the machine which… kinda defeats the purpose of disk encryption, doesn’t it? A thief can break into your house, lift the whole box (they are small these days, huh), plug it in elsewhere, and gain free rein over your files.

So, which one is it? Simplicity and reliability by allowing automatic reboots or data protection by keeping the key in your head? With ZKeys—a little new project I’m launching today—you can have your cake and eat it too.

A blog on operating systems, programming languages, testing, build systems, my own software projects and even personal productivity. Specifics include FreeBSD, Linux, Rust, Bazel and EndBASIC.

ZKeys is a managed service that stores encryption passphrases off-site. Client machines fetch the encryption keys from the service and keep those keys alive (unlocked) via periodic keepalive messages.

If the server stops receiving those messages for a long period of time (e.g. because it has been unplugged as part of theft), access to the key is automatically locked. You get automatic, passwordless reboots while keeping your data protected against real incidents.

About a year ago, I was reinstalling my FreeBSD home server to act as my NAS after a short stint with a Synology box. During that process, I decided to enable encryption for the ZFS data pool. But as I did that, I faced the question: how do I allow the machine to automatically serve my data after a reboot? I could put the key in a local file, which FreeBSD supports via the keylocation ZFS option, but that would render disk encryption pretty much useless.

So I had an idea: what if I put the key on a different machine away from my house? I could then make the NAS fetch the key to mount the drives. And if someone stole the server, I could wipe the remote key and make the data unreadable before they could plug it back in. I did post this to Twitter and there was some interest in the idea:

Silly or smart? I wrote a startup rc.d script to automatically mount my encrypted ZFS pool by fetching the key from a remote server. What I want to protect against is the "drives stolen from home" case. If that happened, I could quickly remove the key. https://t.co/eZle2nJlXW

But… did you notice the problem with what I said above? Wiping the key by hand after noticing something is wrong is problematic because the gap between those two events may be large. What if I’m on a long vacation and I have no means of noticing that the machine has been stolen? Game over.

So I refined the idea: what if I had a service that stored the key remotely and required the consuming machine to send it frequent pings to keep the key unlocked? This way, if the machine were stolen, it’d likely be offline for more than a few minutes and the service could automatically determine that something is wrong and lock the key from future accesses. And that’s precisely what ZKeys does.

Before getting into details, it’s important to get something out of the way early on: ZKeys is designed so that a compromise of the server is insufficient to retrieve or reconstruct your keys. This is done in two ways:

Split keys: The ZKeys server only stores half of the passphrase used to unlock a volume. The other half remains on the client machine. The zkeys client program combines both halves to produce the whole passphrase.

Encryption at rest: The keys are encrypted at rest with a password that the zkeys client must supply for retrieval and which is securely transmitted over HTTPS. The password only lives in the server’s memory for a brief period of time.

The tradeoff is, simply, that you MUST back up the full encryption keys in a third location of your own (a paper in a safe, a password manager, you name it). If ZKeys goes down or you lose your remote key password, there is absolutely nothing I can do to help you. You have been warned. The setup instructions later on will instruct you how to back the key up.

The ZKeys service is available at https://zkeys.jmmv.dev/ and the open-source client—along with system integrations for FreeBSD and Fedora—is in my jmmv/zkeys repository.

Disclaimer: ZKeys is offered to you for free. I make no promises about its reliability, availability, or anything else. I am giving you access because I expect that supporting a handful of extra users won’t be a big issue. But if support becomes a burden, or if load becomes unmanageable, I’ll have to re-evaluate my choices here. Which means that, for now, you have to use the limited LAUNCH50 code to get started!

The basic idea to set ZKeys up is the following:

That’s all it takes, but the steps are quite manual at the moment because no upstream OSes provide packages. The service documentation covers several scenarios in detail for FreeBSD and Fedora, so I’m not going to repeat them here.

Now for the nerdy details, because that’s what you’re here for:

The CLI is Rust, of course, but it has to come with a bunch of shell scripts to integrate with FreeBSD’s rc.d system and Fedora’s systemd and dracut. These integration points are the trickiest part of the client package and the hardest to validate automatically, which is why I only provide support for the two systems I use on a daily basis. It should be possible to integrate ZKeys with any other Unix-like OS, though.

The CLI package is a frankenmonster made up of Cargo and GNU Automake/Autoconf because of the above. I believe system-level tools should abide by de facto standard tooling even if such standard tooling is deficient, so GNU Autotools it is.

I run two non-replicated instances of the service: one in US West and one in EU West, because I have machines in both locations and both rely on encryption. I strongly suggest you use the US instance as I’m seeing frequent power hiccups in my EU deployment.

The frontend uses Bootstrap, straight from the 2010s. Does it look ugly? Maybe, but I kinda like it and, most importantly, it’s functional. Of course I could have asked AI to produce yet-another-dull-site full of gradients and slop, but I did not want to do that, so I stuck to what I already knew how to do by hand from previous services.

The admin page is an SPA, yes. This is an intentional design choice that forces a very clear separation between the backend APIs and the frontend. Whatever the current web frontend can do any other client can do as well. For example: the current zkeys CLI tool does not offer administrative operations but it could trivially be made to support them, because the APIs are right there.

The backend is built on my own III-IV framework which I’ve been building up over the last few years while implementing a couple of other services. I’ve had to add a lot more features to it to make this possible though.

Oops, I know that’s the name of a different effort, but you really need to encrypt your local drives. With computers becoming smaller and lighter every day, it’s easier than ever to steal them, and unfortunately home break-ins are common in some parts of the world. Block-level encryption is cheap in CPU terms, so do it. ZKeys just makes this process less cumbersome for headless servers.

Once again, head to https://zkeys.jmmv.dev/ to get started and use code LAUNCH50 to create an account!

And if you are interested in helping out, I’d appreciate the creation of packages for FreeBSD and Fedora, and integration scripts for other systems.
