A bit about email

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

First, some terminology; A typical email will past through many different components on the way to the recipient:

(These aren't all necessarily different machines or even different programs, but it is helpful to name each step.) The mail user agent (MUA) runs on the user's computer and is what they use to send or read messages. Let's say they send something: that message will be uploaded to to the submission agent (MSA) which checks the user's password before handing off to the mail transfer agent (MTA). The transfer agent's job is to talk to other mail servers and get the message to the receiving side's MTA. Once received, it's given to the local delivery agent (LDA) to be stored until the recipient checks their email. The name "LDA" is non-standard, but I use it to avoid having four different abbreviations differing by a single letter. The reason for this structure is that when the system was being designed (around 1980), most organizations could only afford a single server. Maintenance was a full time job and updates often required multiple days of downtime. A well-connected computer might have dedicated single-digits megabit/second link that worked half the time. This forced the MTA and MUA distinction: if the recipient's server isn't up when the email is set, the sending MTA will save the message and re-attempt delivery later. This way, short outages don't cause bounced mail. Since the mail client is an interactive program the user runs, a separate message store is needed to keep the email until it can be read. Most MTAs are configured to continue retrying a message for five days, usually with exponential backoff: mine will wait 5 minutes after the first failure, and increase the interval to one hour in prolonged outages. The MSA and LDA have always existed in some capacity, but have gotten a lot more complicated when people started accessing email from their own computers instead or remotely logging onto a server. SMTP Most communication happens over the Simple Mail Transfer Protocol, including MTA-MTA delivery. To narrow it down a bit, I'll pretend do be an MTA sending email to blog@maurycyz.com. The first step is to look up the receiving MTA for that domain: # dig MX maurycyz.com [snip] maurycyz.com. 300 IN MX 1 smallcrow.com. [snip] # dig A smallcrow.com [snip] smallcrow.com. 300 IN A 65.109.172.162 [snip] Email is old enough to have it's own dedicated DNS record instead of using subdomain hacks or TXT abuse. The number is a priority (smaller is higher), which is used if multiple hosts are specified: a big email provider will have multiple servers to ensure timely delivery. SMTP is a relatively straightforward text based protocol. The one catch is that most mail servers will require a CRLF as a data terminator. This can often be sent with ctrl-m, but using a tool that automatically inserts cartridge-return bytes is easier: # telnet telnet> set crlf Will send carriage returns as telnet . telnet> open smallcrow.com 25 Trying 65.109.172.162... Connected to smallcrow.com. Escape character is '^]'. 220 smallcrow.com ESMTP Postfix HELO nowhere 250 smallcrow.com MAIL FROM: 250 2.1.0 Ok RCPT TO: 250 2.1.5 Ok DATA 354 End data with . From: To: Hello, world. . 250 2.0.0 Ok: queued as C870D7CD93 QUIT 221 2.0.0 Bye Each line sent is a command, to which the remote server responds with a three digit number followed by a human readable explaintaion. 200 responses mean it worked, 300 is "carry on...", 400 indicates temporary errors which tell the sender to try again later, 500 errors indicate that the email should be returned as un-deliverable. (If this seems similar to HTTP, that's no coincidence. A lot of the web, including its header syntax, was lifted from SMTP) The first command is always HELO (or EHLO), followed the sending MTA's hostname. This is usually not the same hostname that's in an email address, but many providers require it to match the sender's IP address. . The next two, MAIL FROM and RCPT TO specify the address to deliver to, and the address for any failure notifications. Bounce messages are usually generated one step up from where the error occurred: in most cases the receiving MTA has a problem and sends an error code to the sending MTA, but by that time MSA has already disconnect, forcing an emailed notification. Those notification themselves use an empty MAIL FROM to avoid infinite loops. Once the address has been specified and accepted, a DATA command is sent, followed by the actual message. This, at a minimum, contains the "From" and "To" headers along with some text. The message is terminated with a lone period to which the server replies to indicate that the message was sucessfuly handed off. Final hop MTAs should forward the email to the LDA before sending back a status code. This ensures that they never accept a message which they can't deliver, which would result in a error notification. This "backscatter" message interacts badly with spammers that forge MAIL FROM: it turns their obviously bogus message into one that is likely to be delivered and cause a nusance. There is no requirement for the addresses inside the email to match the ones in MAIL FROM and RCPT TO (also known as the envelope). The most common cause of this is a mail that has been forwarded. For example, a mailing list will set the envelope destination to match each member's email, but keep the original "From" and "To" headers intact so that replies work correctly. The envelope from will usually be changed to point to somewhere the list administrator will check for error notifications. Now, it's not that intermediate systems don't modify the headers: Most stages will add a "received" header to record where they got the message. While required for MTA to MTA communication, SMTP is used throughout the whole system. The mail client submits email using a different port, but over the same protocol. Communication between internal components (i.e. MTA to LDA) often uses the closely related Local Mail Transfer Protocol: the main difference is the assumption that the receiver doesn't have a message queue and will either deliver the mail immediately or return an error. Submission usually uses TLS to port 465, with the addition of a password (in base64): AUTH PLAIN 334 AGh1bnRlcgBodW50ZXIy 235 2.7.0 Authentication successful The one thing that's significantly different is the MUA's fetch, which operates on a "pull" basis. I won't go into the syntax here, but it's not terribly complicated. ... but if you actually try sending an email to, say, gmail, based on what I've written so far, it won't work. Some of that is due to them enforcing things like the Message-Id header: a unique identifier used to de-duplicate mail in cases where the receiving MTA accepts an email but the connection drops before the sender is informed about this. This causes the sender to try again later, which duplicating the message. However, a big part of it is... Authentication Base SMTP trusts that every sender is be well behaved: anyone can send email as anyone else. In fact, many hosts were configured to accept and relay mail from anyone as a conveyance for when someone's usual server was down. The original solution to bad behavior was to get the culprit kicked off the internet... but that fell apart quickly once the network grew and companies began getting involved: the first documented case of spam email was sent in 1978 and sold 13 million dollars in computers. While authentication doesn't prevent spam, it makes it possible to filter out unwanted senders. Verifying sender addresses is not possible using the MX record, since there is no guarantee the same host is used for receiving and sending mail. The simplest solution is to publish the sending address in a seperate DNS record: @ TXT v=spf1 ip4:65.109.172.162 ip6:2a01:4f9:c011:841a:b01d::1 -all ; Roughly: ; allow from 65.109.172.162 ; allow from 2a01:4f9:c011:841a:b01d::1 ; deny otherwise However, that's not quite enough for the email empire. Big-tech also wants the email body to be signed with a cryptographic key published via a (DKIM) DNS record. This requires adding a step to sending mails, usually as a plugin for the MTA. While I rag on big-tech a lot, but this is actually a sensible policy: SPF can only verify the MAIL FROM, since forwarding the email will chance the source IP address. DKIM signatures are attached to the message and can be used to verify the original sender (the "From" address) of a message, which is what's actually shown to the user in most cases. However, both of these don't actually prevent spam, because spammers can just set up a few TXT records. Big-tech also requires the sender's HELO hostname to be consistent with their IP address, via both forward and reverse DNS. This helps prevent compromised devices from spewing out junk: a smart TV is unlikely to have a meaningful PTR record, and if it does, that hostname won't resolve back to it's address. ... unfortunately this policy also makes it impossible to directly send mail from most residential internet connections unless you own the address block. Of course, the solution is to pay a big-tech company money to relay your emails. If you don't like this, find a mail provider with more permissive rules: the only reason big-tech is able to keep their racket running that five or so companies control 99% of email. The message This is where SMTP stops being so simple. Let's look at the simplest possible email once it's been delivered: Return-Path: Delivered-To: bob@here Received: from here by here with LMTP id bs05E10S7Go4VQMAHrglgg (envelope-from ) for ; Thu, 05 Nov 2026 05:21:33 +0000 DKIM-Filter: OpenDKIM Filter v2.11.0 here B05E67CD18 Received-SPF: None (mailfrom) identity=mailfrom; client-ip=313.33.33.337; helo=there; envelope-from=alex@there; receiver=here Received: from there (unknown [313.33.333.337]) by here (Postfix) with SMTP id B05E67CD18 for ; Thu, 05 Nov 2026 05:20:42 +0000 (UTC) From: alex@there To: bob@here Hello, World! The format of the headers is superficially similar to HTTP headers, except that no line should exceed 78 bytes. (without the CRLF line terminator) Overlong headers are broken up and newly created lines are prefixed with whitespace to indicate that they are a continuation of the last header. Mail software generally preppends headers to the start of a message, so it's helpful to read them backwards. "From" and "to" are fairly self explanatory. Next, the "received" header contains a note from the receiving MTA: it includes the source's HELO name, IP address, RCPT TO and a timestamp. The MTA then performed SPF and DKIM checks, which came up empty: There's no signature and the MAIL FROM hostname is not publically resolvable. The LDA added another "received" header, although it's not very helpful. More importantly, it tacked on the "return-path" with the original MAIL FROM address and recorded the mailbox the message was delivered to. However, the body isn't always plain text. This is an email that includes formatting: [...] MIME-Version: 1.0 Content-Type: multipart/alternative; boundary=----IX71FHL6PHA0538LS1WKT2W9SF7S8X Content-Transfer-Encoding: 7bit ------IX71FHL6PHA0538LS1WKT2W9SF7S8X Content-Type: text/plain; charset=utf-8 Content-Transfer-Encoding: quoted-printable Hello, World! I can do bold text! ------IX71FHL6PHA0538LS1WKT2W9SF7S8X Content-Type: text/html; charset=utf-8 Content-Transfer-Encoding: quoted-printable

Hello, world! I can do bold<= /b> text
------IX71FHL6PHA0538LS1WKT2W9SF7S8X-- That's right, it's a web page! However, since not all email clients can understand HTML, the message also includes a plain text version. This is done using MIME's "multipart" encoding, in this case, multipart/alternative, which indicates all the versions are functionally equivalent. MIME's version number has been stuck at 1.0 since 1996. The delimiter is a randomly chosen string, which allows multiple multiparts to be nested inside without escaping. Each section of the message has it's own headers, in this case, indicating that it's sent in the quoted-printable encoding. This allows the email message to contain any bytes without exposing the MTA to scary things like non-seven-bit ASCII: remember, the SMTP is 45 years old. In quoted-printable, equal signs are used as the escape character: if followed by two hex digits, it decodes to that byte. If followed by a newline, both characters are removed. This is used to break long lines that could otherwise cause trouble. In this email, "=3D" becomes a literal equal sign and both lines are joined together. If you've ever seen an email with stray equals or missing one or two characters ~ every 80 bytes, broken quoted-printable decoding is usually the culprit. The second "content-transfer-encoding" is base64, which is used for binary files like attached images: MIME-Version: 1.0 Content-Type: multipart/mixed; boundary="5ZTNvId/H+rlk7Fr" Content-Disposition: inline --5ZTNvId/H+rlk7Fr Content-Type: text/plain; charset=us-ascii Content-Disposition: inline Check out this file: --5ZTNvId/H+rlk7Fr Content-Type: application/octet-stream Content-Disposition: attachment; filename="file.bin" Content-Transfer-Encoding: base64 J7mtSdbQItOAi345nwaNkkTXrnDIRPA0DSwhbSAOwm2C/KoHjQIotY9lnJRMBRk9InQaHOxL bb7DGd9EN6uRpjvCbox9xblAh3CxH7sl+YAkYcKw1V100RpBwMhgaXfUJBB3oRkLffgRwEyK v9qFBLp9zZ+SiRJoRWbA4aZLexPhHZ35vFROVKYtiAeMlD1s15v3kPZvEADam0e45BoSaqmp 1S3z3b5wBIFHJUxQULD589MKaFx2Z2A+91pl/pbZWmZkzt55D/hZ05kjUUbXUnDnTYAQE2Gk WnN+SnrQvfEO+Tz7AdrbLD1q2DgwWW+k0cVJLKjdwRxesGhAkQCZfHVrno6R4iXjTbInIbQv hXzQiV840iUiUhk282WADd1ffJ0sot2Waf7XHLIE1lZVFQ3iGofYbw8pZlg3sb35AnIWPZKv fbC6Yh95RV23R4K2NHeBX1rpl4bSSiftnD2RbsBzv7cgw/6M/Lj3O40NFGAaUC+J7TpAaXdi tvlPsNQhMo8QRf/ub7WK42cPUlz1fKq1k1pzGN96YOsE5azMfF4vLANtbtbVmLIp2WmgBk0P 9IGMWy/xIAe0cOh+bqzru2+ojJGgx9uX4VSTP9BaTOwkVX5+rQfpp1qJodtHiO8sHw6Pg+Mg WcpqQPC7118ccuT44cVpIRkwOSoJzsW++WM= --5ZTNvId/H+rlk7Fr-- This is why size limits are often disappointing: the email will be 33% larger than the attached file. More efficient encodings (raw binary data) are supported by some MTAs, but may have to be converted to base64 somewhere down the path... which would break DKIM signing. Generally: No encoding ("7-bit") can be used if the message has short lines and fits in low-bit ASCII. However, "7-bit" is mostly seen as a null encoding for already encoded multipart containers. Quoted-printable if the message is mostly text, but has something that risks breaking ancient software. Base64 for binary data. About TLS The SSL/TLS used by mail clients is effectively the same as for a browser: the mail provider must go to a certificate authority, prove their identity and get a certificate.. Algorithms and ciphers are disabled at the slightest hint they may be insecure. However, MTAs operates on "trusted" networks... although it's less that an attacker can't get access to internet traffic, but that if they could, it would be possible for them to pass an ACME challenge and take out a trusted certificate. As a result, the goal is mostly to protect against passive eavesdropping: most sysadmins don't bother dealing with a CA and use self-signed certificates. (it's not like CAs have a good track record: 4) Another weird thing about email is that MTAs don't use a separate TLS port. All connections on port 25 start as plaintext, then the sender issues a STARTTLS command and hands over the connection to a SSL/TLS library. It's somewhat janky, but it allows servers to attempt to use encryption and cleanly fall back if it's not supported. Such an approach isn't great for submissions, since a fall back would leak the password... and baddies on shared networks or nosy residential ISPs can strip out the STARTTLS command. For this case, it's best to default use the dedicated SSL submission port: 465. (This port actually has a bit of a history, with it being deprecated in favor of STARTTLS before being re-endorsed once we realized using optional SSL for passwords was a bad idea.) So what?? Unless you write email related software or run infrastructure, most of this information is profoundly useless. However, there are a few things you might want to remember. The underlying protocols allow five days for delivery, and it can sometimes take longer. Since users expect delivery in seconds and most emails are at least somewhat time sensitive, some MTAs are configured to send out delay notification if a message is taking a while. You may want to find out if yours does this before assuming the recipient is ignoring you. Emails are digital postcards. A message will be stored and forwarded by multiple systems in plaintext. Anyone on the network path may be able to read your email: SSL/TLS is not universally supported and can trivially be stripped out by active attackers. This is an inherent part of email: there's no way to tack-on strong encryption without breaking existing clients. If you need a guarantee of privacy, you must encrypt the message before sending, and the recipient must decrypt it themselves. Be careful with built-in tools like PGP: these generally make it way too easy to accidentally hit "reply-all" without encrypting, in addition to other flaws like exposing the subject line. PGP stuff Since publication, multiple people have written to me explaining how obfuscating the subject line (or warning if the user fills it out) is completly unreasonable to expect from a for a self-proclaimed privacy tool. At least in my eyes, "encrypted messaging" means encrypted, not 90% encrypted and 10% plaintext. The lack of deniablity and forward secrecy can be excused as limitiations of email's store-and-forward architecture... but default plaintext and making no attempt to encrypt certain the subject line or attachments is a recipe for disaster: at least with vanilla email, people know it is not encrypted. "Encrypted" email providers are largely snake oil. While the message store can encrypt messages and a custom MUA could decrypt them, the MTA must have access to plaintext. As a result, it provides minimal to no security against most attack vectors despite the "millitary grade encryption". The only way to fix email would be to get everyone to agree to switch to an end-to-end encrypted protocol... but the whole point of email is universal compatability: encrypted messaging can exist, but encrypted email is contradictory. The best hope is to find a mail provider that can be trusted not to snoop messages. That is to say, not an Advertising company (google) or one that's been pushing hard for LLMs (microsoft / apple): both of these businesses create strong incentives to read your messages. Although you'll still have to hope whoever you are communicating with isn't beign snooped on by their provider. Worse, DKIM signatures act as a lifelong proof that an email came from your provider's servers, even if a message is years old and has no clear chain of custody. This makes leaked, hacked, or snooped emails ideal extortion material: since the bad actor can trivially prove they haven't been fabricated. This property, called non-repudiation, is generally not desirable for private messaging systems. Avoiding signing not an option if you want to interoperate with big-tech, but it is possible to regularly rotate and publish private keys. Before they key is published, the signature can be trusted to verify it is legitimate. However, once the key has been published, the signature can been forged by anyone and is rendered retroactively untrustworthy. Since authentication is checked during delivery by the MTA and LDA, this doesn't affect how the email will be handled. However, it will cast doubt on the authenticity of leaked messages since the signature could have been forged with minimal effort. While key rotation is common practice, most mail providers don't publish their old private keys, so the signature is still trustworthy (modulo the service being hacked). I would advise against emailing something you wouldn't be comfortable reading out in public. Related: https://www.rfc-editor.org/info/rfc821/: Original SMTP https://www.rfc-editor.org/info/rfc2045/: MIME extended message format https://www.rfc-editor.org/info/rfc7208/: SPF Authentication https://www.rfc-editor.org/info/rfc6376/: DKIM signing

(These aren't all necessarily different machines or even different programs, but it is helpful to name each step.)

The mail user agent (MUA) runs on the user's computer and is what they use to send or read messages. Let's say they send something: that message will be uploaded to to the submission agent (MSA) which checks the user's password before handing off to the mail transfer agent (MTA).

The transfer agent's job is to talk to other mail servers and get the message to the receiving side's MTA. Once received, it's given to the local delivery agent (LDA) to be stored until the recipient checks their email. The name "LDA" is non-standard, but I use it to avoid having four different abbreviations differing by a single letter.

The reason for this structure is that when the system was being designed (around 1980), most organizations could only afford a single server. Maintenance was a full time job and updates often required multiple days of downtime. A well-connected computer might have dedicated single-digits megabit/second link that worked half the time.

This forced the MTA and MUA distinction: if the recipient's server isn't up when the email is set, the sending MTA will save the message and re-attempt delivery later. This way, short outages don't cause bounced mail. Since the mail client is an interactive program the user runs, a separate message store is needed to keep the email until it can be read.

Most MTAs are configured to continue retrying a message for five days, usually with exponential backoff: mine will wait 5 minutes after the first failure, and increase the interval to one hour in prolonged outages.

The MSA and LDA have always existed in some capacity, but have gotten a lot more complicated when people started accessing email from their own computers instead or remotely logging onto a server.

Most communication happens over the Simple Mail Transfer Protocol, including MTA-MTA delivery. To narrow it down a bit, I'll pretend do be an MTA sending email to blog@maurycyz.com.

The first step is to look up the receiving MTA for that domain:

Email is old enough to have it's own dedicated DNS record instead of using subdomain hacks or TXT abuse. The number is a priority (smaller is higher), which is used if multiple hosts are specified: a big email provider will have multiple servers to ensure timely delivery.

SMTP is a relatively straightforward text based protocol. The one catch is that most mail servers will require a CRLF as a data terminator. This can often be sent with ctrl-m, but using a tool that automatically inserts cartridge-return bytes is easier:

Each line sent is a command, to which the remote server responds with a three digit number followed by a human readable explaintaion. 200 responses mean it worked, 300 is "carry on...", 400 indicates temporary errors which tell the sender to try again later, 500 errors indicate that the email should be returned as un-deliverable.

(If this seems similar to HTTP, that's no coincidence. A lot of the web, including its header syntax, was lifted from SMTP)

The first command is always HELO (or EHLO), followed the sending MTA's hostname. This is usually not the same hostname that's in an email address, but many providers require it to match the sender's IP address. . The next two, MAIL FROM and RCPT TO specify the address to deliver to, and the address for any failure notifications. Bounce messages are usually generated one step up from where the error occurred: in most cases the receiving MTA has a problem and sends an error code to the sending MTA, but by that time MSA has already disconnect, forcing an emailed notification. Those notification themselves use an empty MAIL FROM to avoid infinite loops.

Once the address has been specified and accepted, a DATA command is sent, followed by the actual message. This, at a minimum, contains the "From" and "To" headers along with some text. The message is terminated with a lone period to which the server replies to indicate that the message was sucessfuly handed off.

Final hop MTAs should forward the email to the LDA before sending back a status code. This ensures that they never accept a message which they can't deliver, which would result in a error notification. This "backscatter" message interacts badly with spammers that forge MAIL FROM: it turns their obviously bogus message into one that is likely to be delivered and cause a nusance.

There is no requirement for the addresses inside the email to match the ones in MAIL FROM and RCPT TO (also known as the envelope). The most common cause of this is a mail that has been forwarded. For example, a mailing list will set the envelope destination to match each member's email, but keep the original "From" and "To" headers intact so that replies work correctly. The envelope from will usually be changed to point to somewhere the list administrator will check for error notifications.

Now, it's not that intermediate systems don't modify the headers: Most stages will add a "received" header to record where they got the message.

While required for MTA to MTA communication, SMTP is used throughout the whole system. The mail client submits email using a different port, but over the same protocol. Communication between internal components (i.e. MTA to LDA) often uses the closely related Local Mail Transfer Protocol: the main difference is the assumption that the receiver doesn't have a message queue and will either deliver the mail immediately or return an error.

Submission usually uses TLS to port 465, with the addition of a password (in base64):

AUTH PLAIN 334 AGh1bnRlcgBodW50ZXIy 235 2.7.0 Authentication successful

The one thing that's significantly different is the MUA's fetch, which operates on a "pull" basis. I won't go into the syntax here, but it's not terribly complicated.

... but if you actually try sending an email to, say, gmail, based on what I've written so far, it won't work. Some of that is due to them enforcing things like the Message-Id header: a unique identifier used to de-duplicate mail in cases where the receiving MTA accepts an email but the connection drops before the sender is informed about this. This causes the sender to try again later, which duplicating the message.

Base SMTP trusts that every sender is be well behaved: anyone can send email as anyone else. In fact, many hosts were configured to accept and relay mail from anyone as a conveyance for when someone's usual server was down.

The original solution to bad behavior was to get the culprit kicked off the internet... but that fell apart quickly once the network grew and companies began getting involved: the first documented case of spam email was sent in 1978 and sold 13 million dollars in computers. While authentication doesn't prevent spam, it makes it possible to filter out unwanted senders.

Verifying sender addresses is not possible using the MX record, since there is no guarantee the same host is used for receiving and sending mail. The simplest solution is to publish the sending address in a seperate DNS record:

@ TXT v=spf1 ip4:65.109.172.162 ip6:2a01:4f9:c011:841a:b01d::1 -all ; Roughly: ; allow from 65.109.172.162 ; allow from 2a01:4f9:c011:841a:b01d::1 ; deny otherwise

However, that's not quite enough for the email empire. Big-tech also wants the email body to be signed with a cryptographic key published via a (DKIM) DNS record. This requires adding a step to sending mails, usually as a plugin for the MTA.

While I rag on big-tech a lot, but this is actually a sensible policy: SPF can only verify the MAIL FROM, since forwarding the email will chance the source IP address. DKIM signatures are attached to the message and can be used to verify the original sender (the "From" address) of a message, which is what's actually shown to the user in most cases.

However, both of these don't actually prevent spam, because spammers can just set up a few TXT records. Big-tech also requires the sender's HELO hostname to be consistent with their IP address, via both forward and reverse DNS. This helps prevent compromised devices from spewing out junk: a smart TV is unlikely to have a meaningful PTR record, and if it does, that hostname won't resolve back to it's address.

... unfortunately this policy also makes it impossible to directly send mail from most residential internet connections unless you own the address block. Of course, the solution is to pay a big-tech company money to relay your emails. If you don't like this, find a mail provider with more permissive rules: the only reason big-tech is able to keep their racket running that five or so companies control 99% of email.

This is where SMTP stops being so simple. Let's look at the simplest possible email once it's been delivered:

Return-Path: Delivered-To: bob@here Received: from here by here with LMTP id bs05E10S7Go4VQMAHrglgg (envelope-from ) for ; Thu, 05 Nov 2026 05:21:33 +0000 DKIM-Filter: OpenDKIM Filter v2.11.0 here B05E67CD18 Received-SPF: None (mailfrom) identity=mailfrom; client-ip=313.33.33.337; helo=there; envelope-from=alex@there; receiver=here Received: from there (unknown [313.33.333.337]) by here (Postfix) with SMTP id B05E67CD18 for ; Thu, 05 Nov 2026 05:20:42 +0000 (UTC) From: alex@there To: bob@here Hello, World!

The format of the headers is superficially similar to HTTP headers, except that no line should exceed 78 bytes. (without the CRLF line terminator) Overlong headers are broken up and newly created lines are prefixed with whitespace to indicate that they are a continuation of the last header.

Mail software generally preppends headers to the start of a message, so it's helpful to read them backwards. "From" and "to" are fairly self explanatory. Next, the "received" header contains a note from the receiving MTA: it includes the source's HELO name, IP address, RCPT TO and a timestamp.

The MTA then performed SPF and DKIM checks, which came up empty: There's no signature and the MAIL FROM hostname is not publically resolvable.

The LDA added another "received" header, although it's not very helpful. More importantly, it tacked on the "return-path" with the original MAIL FROM address and recorded the mailbox the message was delivered to.

However, the body isn't always plain text. This is an email that includes formatting:

[...] MIME-Version: 1.0 Content-Type: multipart/alternative; boundary=----IX71FHL6PHA0538LS1WKT2W9SF7S8X Content-Transfer-Encoding: 7bit ------IX71FHL6PHA0538LS1WKT2W9SF7S8X Content-Type: text/plain; charset=utf-8 Content-Transfer-Encoding: quoted-printable Hello, World! I can do bold text! ------IX71FHL6PHA0538LS1WKT2W9SF7S8X Content-Type: text/html; charset=utf-8 Content-Transfer-Encoding: quoted-printable

Hello, world! I can do bold<= /b> text
------IX71FHL6PHA0538LS1WKT2W9SF7S8X--

However, since not all email clients can understand HTML, the message also includes a plain text version. This is done using MIME's "multipart" encoding, in this case, multipart/alternative, which indicates all the versions are functionally equivalent.

MIME's version number has been stuck at 1.0 since 1996.

The delimiter is a randomly chosen string, which allows multiple multiparts to be nested inside without escaping. Each section of the message has it's own headers, in this case, indicating that it's sent in the quoted-printable encoding. This allows the email message to contain any bytes without exposing the MTA to scary things like non-seven-bit ASCII: remember, the SMTP is 45 years old.

In quoted-printable, equal signs are used as the escape character: if followed by two hex digits, it decodes to that byte. If followed by a newline, both characters are removed. This is used to break long lines that could otherwise cause trouble.

In this email, "=3D" becomes a literal equal sign and both lines are joined together. If you've ever seen an email with stray equals or missing one or two characters ~ every 80 bytes, broken quoted-printable decoding is usually the culprit.

The second "content-transfer-encoding" is base64, which is used for binary files like attached images:

MIME-Version: 1.0 Content-Type: multipart/mixed; boundary="5ZTNvId/H+rlk7Fr" Content-Disposition: inline --5ZTNvId/H+rlk7Fr Content-Type: text/plain; charset=us-ascii Content-Disposition: inline Check out this file: --5ZTNvId/H+rlk7Fr Content-Type: application/octet-stream Content-Disposition: attachment; filename="file.bin" Content-Transfer-Encoding: base64 J7mtSdbQItOAi345nwaNkkTXrnDIRPA0DSwhbSAOwm2C/KoHjQIotY9lnJRMBRk9InQaHOxL bb7DGd9EN6uRpjvCbox9xblAh3CxH7sl+YAkYcKw1V100RpBwMhgaXfUJBB3oRkLffgRwEyK v9qFBLp9zZ+SiRJoRWbA4aZLexPhHZ35vFROVKYtiAeMlD1s15v3kPZvEADam0e45BoSaqmp 1S3z3b5wBIFHJUxQULD589MKaFx2Z2A+91pl/pbZWmZkzt55D/hZ05kjUUbXUnDnTYAQE2Gk WnN+SnrQvfEO+Tz7AdrbLD1q2DgwWW+k0cVJLKjdwRxesGhAkQCZfHVrno6R4iXjTbInIbQv hXzQiV840iUiUhk282WADd1ffJ0sot2Waf7XHLIE1lZVFQ3iGofYbw8pZlg3sb35AnIWPZKv fbC6Yh95RV23R4K2NHeBX1rpl4bSSiftnD2RbsBzv7cgw/6M/Lj3O40NFGAaUC+J7TpAaXdi tvlPsNQhMo8QRf/ub7WK42cPUlz1fKq1k1pzGN96YOsE5azMfF4vLANtbtbVmLIp2WmgBk0P 9IGMWy/xIAe0cOh+bqzru2+ojJGgx9uX4VSTP9BaTOwkVX5+rQfpp1qJodtHiO8sHw6Pg+Mg WcpqQPC7118ccuT44cVpIRkwOSoJzsW++WM= --5ZTNvId/H+rlk7Fr--

This is why size limits are often disappointing: the email will be 33% larger than the attached file. More efficient encodings (raw binary data) are supported by some MTAs, but may have to be converted to base64 somewhere down the path... which would break DKIM signing.

No encoding ("7-bit") can be used if the message has short lines and fits in low-bit ASCII. However, "7-bit" is mostly seen as a null encoding for already encoded multipart containers. Quoted-printable if the message is mostly text, but has something that risks breaking ancient software. Base64 for binary data.

The SSL/TLS used by mail clients is effectively the same as for a browser: the mail provider must go to a certificate authority, prove their identity and get a certificate.. Algorithms and ciphers are disabled at the slightest hint they may be insecure.

However, MTAs operates on "trusted" networks... although it's less that an attacker can't get access to internet traffic, but that if they could, it would be possible for them to pass an ACME challenge and take out a trusted certificate.

As a result, the goal is mostly to protect against passive eavesdropping: most sysadmins don't bother dealing with a CA and use self-signed certificates. (it's not like CAs have a good track record: 4)

Another weird thing about email is that MTAs don't use a separate TLS port. All connections on port 25 start as plaintext, then the sender issues a STARTTLS command and hands over the connection to a SSL/TLS library. It's somewhat janky, but it allows servers to attempt to use encryption and cleanly fall back if it's not supported.

Such an approach isn't great for submissions, since a fall back would leak the password... and baddies on shared networks or nosy residential ISPs can strip out the STARTTLS command. For this case, it's best to default use the dedicated SSL submission port: 465.

(This port actually has a bit of a history, with it being deprecated in favor of STARTTLS before being re-endorsed once we realized using optional SSL for passwords was a bad idea.)

Unless you write email related software or run infrastructure, most of this information is profoundly useless. However, there are a few things you might want to remember.

The underlying protocols allow five days for delivery, and it can sometimes take longer. Since users expect delivery in seconds and most emails are at least somewhat time sensitive, some MTAs are configured to send out delay notification if a message is taking a while.

You may want to find out if yours does this before assuming the recipient is ignoring you.

A message will be stored and forwarded by multiple systems in plaintext. Anyone on the network path may be able to read your email: SSL/TLS is not universally supported and can trivially be stripped out by active attackers.

This is an inherent part of email: there's no way to tack-on strong encryption without breaking existing clients. If you need a guarantee of privacy, you must encrypt the message before sending, and the recipient must decrypt it themselves. Be careful with built-in tools like PGP: these generally make it way too easy to accidentally hit "reply-all" without encrypting, in addition to other flaws like exposing the subject line.

Since publication, multiple people have written to me explaining how obfuscating the subject line (or warning if the user fills it out) is completly unreasonable to expect from a for a self-proclaimed privacy tool. At least in my eyes, "encrypted messaging" means encrypted, not 90% encrypted and 10% plaintext.

The lack of deniablity and forward secrecy can be excused as limitiations of email's store-and-forward architecture... but default plaintext and making no attempt to encrypt certain the subject line or attachments is a recipe for disaster: at least with vanilla email, people know it is not encrypted.

"Encrypted" email providers are largely snake oil. While the message store can encrypt messages and a custom MUA could decrypt them, the MTA must have access to plaintext. As a result, it provides minimal to no security against most attack vectors despite the "millitary grade encryption". The only way to fix email would be to get everyone to agree to switch to an end-to-end encrypted protocol... but the whole point of email is universal compatability: encrypted messaging can exist, but encrypted email is contradictory.

The best hope is to find a mail provider that can be trusted not to snoop messages. That is to say, not an Advertising company (google) or one that's been pushing hard for LLMs (microsoft / apple): both of these businesses create strong incentives to read your messages. Although you'll still have to hope whoever you are communicating with isn't beign snooped on by their provider.

Worse, DKIM signatures act as a lifelong proof that an email came from your provider's servers, even if a message is years old and has no clear chain of custody. This makes leaked, hacked, or snooped emails ideal extortion material: since the bad actor can trivially prove they haven't been fabricated.

This property, called non-repudiation, is generally not desirable for private messaging systems. Avoiding signing not an option if you want to interoperate with big-tech, but it is possible to regularly rotate and publish private keys. Before they key is published, the signature can be trusted to verify it is legitimate. However, once the key has been published, the signature can been forged by anyone and is rendered retroactively untrustworthy.

Since authentication is checked during delivery by the MTA and LDA, this doesn't affect how the email will be handled. However, it will cast doubt on the authenticity of leaked messages since the signature could have been forged with minimal effort.

While key rotation is common practice, most mail providers don't publish their old private keys, so the signature is still trustworthy (modulo the service being hacked). I would advise against emailing something you wouldn't be comfortable reading out in public.

InfoQ Homepage News Cloudflare Scales D1 Control Plane to Millions of Databases

Nov 08, 2026 2 min read

In a recent article, Cloudflare shared the redesign of the control plane of its D1 serverless SQL database to support millions of databases, avoiding centralized bottlenecks, implementing retries and idempotency, and using eventual consistency.

The D1 control plane architecture is built around Postgres for transactional metadata, ClickHouse for analytics, and Workers KV for globally replicated routing data. It keeps query processing at the edge, separate from centralized management operations, using distributed metadata caching, connection pooling, asynchronous workflows, and periodic health checks to improve scalability and recover from failures. Announced in 2022, Cloudflare D1 is a serverless SQL database built on SQLite and designed to run close to applications on Cloudflare’s global network. Over the past year, the number of daily active databases has grown 18-fold.

Cloudflare uses 30-second heartbeats, with per-database jitter, to reconcile each D1 Durable Object with Postgres, the source of truth for database existence and account limits. This mechanism makes failed creation or deletion operations self-correcting: orphaned objects are marked as deleted, and Workers KV routing metadata is repaired if best-effort updates fail. David Laban, full stack software engineer at Cloudflare, and Lambros Petrou, principal engineer at Cloudflare, write:

Heartbeats can tolerate slightly out-of-date data, meaning they can be served by Hyperdrive’s multiple caching layers or by a Postgres read replica. This heartbeat flow enables the Durable Object to self-correct to reflect the source of truth (Postgres) regarding the creation and deletion of databases.

Cloudflare keeps D1 queries off its centralized Postgres database by routing requests from an Edge API Worker through an Eyeball Worker directly to the relevant D1 Durable Object.

The Edge API Worker handles authentication, authorization, rate limiting, and request validation, while the Eyeball Worker trusts the database-specific authorization encoded in D1 Bindings, avoiding redundant checks in the query path. Laban and Petrou add:

You might also wonder why we use Workers KV when we already have Hyperdrive Caching. Workers KV is a durable datastore, not just a cache: if an item falls out of KV's edge cache, the read is served from KV's own storage rather than Postgres, keeping Postgres out of the data plane. In a catastrophic cache purge scenario, a million databases trying to fetch their routing information would lead to metastable failures.

Cloudflare enforces D1 storage limits using asynchronous usage updates, which are queued and batched to protect Postgres from write spikes. It maintains pre-aggregated account totals through incremental storage deltas, avoiding expensive scans and write contention for accounts with millions of databases. Durable Objects check these totals through Hyperdrive’s short-lived cache and block writes that would exceed the account limit. In a Reddit thread, user isaackogan comments:

It's an impressive architecture to be sure, but most of these gains are that it's an in-process database running on edge storage near you. The rest is orchestration around that. Cloudflare can leverage their massive global datacenter presence to do something that would be traditionally impossible for small shops, aka economies of scale at work.

In a previous popular thread, several Reddit users argued that D1 is unreliable, raising concerns about its availability and stability in production workloads.

Cloudflare D1 offers a permanent free tier that includes 5 GB of total storage, 5 million rows read per day, and 100,000 rows written per day. Beyond the free tier, storage costs $0.75 USD per GB-month, reads cost $0.001 per million rows, and writes cost $1 USD per million rows. D1 has no compute or data-transfer charges.

InfoQ Homepage News Google Cloud Adds Self-Managed VMware Cloud Foundation on Bare Metal

Nov 08, 2026 3 min read

Google Cloud has announced general availability of a self-managed VMware solution that runs VMware Cloud Foundation (VCF) 9.x on dedicated bare metal, giving customers root access across ESXi, vCenter, and NSX in 22 regions. It sits alongside the Google-managed VMware Engine and follows similar customer-operated offerings from AWS and Oracle.

With the self-managed solution, Google now offers two models that differ in who operates the stack. VMware Engine, the existing service, runs VCF 5.2 on ve1 and ve2 node families, grants role-based vCenter privileges, and keeps lifecycle management with Google. The new option runs VCF 9.x on z3-highmem-192-highlssd-metal instances, each with 96 physical cores on 4th-generation Intel Xeon processors, 1.5 TB of memory, 72 TB of local NVMe and 200 Gbps networking, with customers or their partners driving upgrades and patching through VCF Operations and bringing their own portable VCF subscriptions.

Group product manager Sunny Dua and director of product management Prashanth Bungale wrote:

The launch of VMware Cloud Foundation 9.x by Broadcom is a key driver behind this self-managed deployment option.

In addition, Abhay Kumar, who runs hyperscaler relationships in Broadcom's VCF division, added that license portability lets customers move existing subscriptions and run workloads unchanged.

With the self-managed option, Google provisions the environment and then steps back. According to the deployment documentation, a VMware solution toolkit, driven from the console or Terraform, provisions the bare-metal ESXi nodes with spread placement, configures VMkernel interfaces and Cloud DNS records, and deploys the VCF Installer appliance with a pre-validated configuration. Before any of that, the project must be enabled for VLAN-based networking, the VPC needs an 8896-byte MTU, and management, vSAN, vMotion, and two NSX subnets must exist. From the installer onward, the environment is the customer’s, which is both the point of the model and what it costs.

Storage is vSAN on the local NVMe, with NetApp Volumes supported as an external datastore. The announcement cites "up to 40%" lower server TCO from NVMe memory tiering and "up to 39%" lower storage TCO from vSAN ESA, figures presented without a baseline or methodology, and the only customer quoted, GXO Logistics, describes an early assessment rather than production use.

With this release, Google joins Oracle and AWS in offering customer-operated VCF on bare metal. Oracle Cloud VMware Solution has provided customer-controlled vSphere since 2020, while AWS launched Amazon Elastic VMware Service (EVS) in 2025 and added VCF 9.0 and 9.1 in July. Azure VMware Solution, by contrast, remains a Microsoft-operated service. The managed model lets the provider absorb Broadcom’s release cadence and licensing changes; the self-managed model hands both back, in exchange for root access and the ability to run the current VCF major version rather than the one the provider has certified.

Consultancy Xtravirt, one of fifteen named launch partners, expects that trade to decide adoption, writing in a pre-launch assessment:

Full control over your VCF estate is only worth having if someone in your organisation wants that responsibility. For plenty of organisations, the answer will be yes. For others, it’ll be: "we want the flexibility, but we don’t want to be the ones doing the patching at 2am."

Google's answer is the partner list itself: Accenture, HCL, IBM, Infosys, TCS and Wipro are among those offering managed operations on top of the customer-owned stack.

The announcement gives no pricing, no SLA, no list of the 22 regions, and names no migration path from VMware Engine’s VCF 5.2 to the self-managed 9.x stack. Google says more regions will follow in upcoming quarters and points customers to its account teams or an authorized partner to get started.

InfoQ Homepage News Entire's Git Forge Rethinks Git Collaboration for Coding Agents

Nov 08, 2026 3 min read

Entire is a Git forge designed specifically for collaboration between human developers and AI coding agents. Its premise is that traditional software development infrastructure, particularly the pull request (PR) model, is not well suited to an environment where AI agents generate, review, and iterate on code at high speed.

As Thomas Dohmke, former GitHub CEO and currently CEO of Entire, explains, GitHub and GitLab transformed human-to-human collaboration by centering it around asynchronous code reviews and version control. A multi-agent forge, by contrast, centers around the asynchronous orchestration, versioning, and auditing of AI agents working alongside humans.

Built on top of a fully distributed architecture that enables pushes up to 89 times faster than GitHub, Entire's core component is trails, which addresses the challenges AI coding agents pose to the traditional PR-based development model:

The pull request [timeline] has become an audit log that only the agent reads. It does not provide the information for the developer to decide whether they can merge with confidence.

Trails bring the most relevant information to the top of the page in the form of eval scores that are updated on every push. These scores help developers assess whether a PR has reached a stable state or requires another iteration of agent-driven coding and review:

Only when the confidence is high, risk is low, and all findings are cleared, the merge should happen.

A trail can be initiated from a bug report, a feature request, or manually, and can span multiple repositories. Whenever new code is pushed, a review agent running in a cloud sandbox evaluates the changes and returns a score and a rationale based on code quality, security, slop, and whether human code review is required. For example, if the slop score is orange, developers can ask their coding agent to improve the code and push a new change, thus triggering a new iteration.

The last 5 runs and the current run are shown for each eval. We believe this is crucial as one-shot loops are often missing a critical issue. Repeating the loops and stabilizing the scores is crucial for the success of agentic coding.

Evaluation agents can be configured through Entire's interface or repository-level JSON configuration.

Review agents can suggest fixes or improvements, which remain visible in the trail while it is open. All review findings must be either resolved or closed before a merge is allowed. Developers can also configure repository-level Gates to define the conditions required for approval.

Another foundational concept introduced by Entire is continuous memory, which preserves all accumulated knowledge in the repository so that agents can reuse it across sessions rather than starting from scratch. Continuous memory is implemented by two open-source tools, entire-graph, which builds a precomputed map of the Git repository including ranked code searches, definition and callers, types and routes, and more; and entire-brain, which creates a persistent memory layer by combining the codebase map with previous agent sessions and information recorded in Git history.

This allows agents to always have the right context at the right time, aka the "why" behind the "what changed."

As a final note on Entire, it also supports integrations with CI platforms like Buildkite and Depot.

Commenting on the announcement on Hacker News, user conartist6 expressed the view that engineers have delegated writing code to agents because of programming languages and tools that leave much to be desired. Haimantika, currently at Entire, points out that the problem has never been languages or tools, rather

it' s that the reasoning gets thrown away between the person and the artifact. Commit messages were always bad, docs always rotted, “why is this here” was always answered by whoever remembered. Agents just made that acute, because now there's no one who remembers. That's the bit we're trying to fix. If a better language fixes it too, honestly I'd take that.

Entire is only one of several new players in the source code management arena, which also include Zed DeltaDB, Anthropic Scion, East River Source Control, Cursor Origin, Radicle, and others.

Presented by: David Iparraguirre - Senior Product Marketing Manager at Datadog, and Jessica Yang - Product Manager at Datadog

The Repair Order in the Browser Subtitle Editor: Four Checkboxes, One Sequence, and a Measured Shift

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

I work on OnePassApps - this is our own build, and every number below was measured on it today.

The Subtitle Workbench does not leave its timing behaviour to guesswork. Under the heading "Optional repairs" the page prints four checkboxes and, above them, the words "Applied in this order": Sort by start time, Clamp timings before zero, Fix zero or negative durations, Move overlapping cues later.

The order is the interesting part, because the panel also states what each repair is allowed to change. One line of contract text sits under the checkboxes:

Overlap repair works chronologically and preserves durations; only Sort changes cue order. Overlap warnings alone do not block export.

That is three separate claims - that the overlap repair walks the cues in time order, that moving a cue does not stretch it, and that exactly one of the four repairs reorders anything. And the fourth sentence says an overlap is a warning, not an error: it does not stop the export.

I opened the app in Chrome, loaded its built-in example, ticked all four repairs and applied a +1,200 ms shift.

Before the apply, the source stage read "example.srt 2 cues · 137 B · Sample data · OK", the file counter read "1 of 20", and the result header read "2 cues · 0:01–0:06". The two cues ran 00:00:01,000 → 00:00:03,000 and 00:00:04,000 → 00:00:06,000, both 2.0 s long.

After the apply, the result header read "2 cues · 0:02–0:07" and the cues ran 00:00:02,200 → 00:00:04,200 and 00:00:05,200 → 00:00:07,200. Both moved by exactly 1,200 ms and both kept their 2.0 s duration. The badge under the header read "2 cues", "No issues", "Shift +1,200 ms" and "4 repairs", and the status line said "Timing changes applied to every loaded file. Originals are unchanged."

Two details are worth pulling out of that. The badge read "4 repairs" on a two-cue file, the same count as the boxes I had ticked - I did not find a label on the page that says what the number counts, so I am only reporting what it showed. And the shift landed on the original cue times rather than on top of a previous shift: each cue moved by exactly 1,200 ms and no more. The app's own guide makes the same promise in its own words - timing changes start from the original cues rather than accumulating silently - and this run is consistent with it.

The cue list carries its own keyboard contract, printed under the cues:

Up and Down move between cues. Enter edits. Left bracket moves the in point earlier and right bracket moves the out point later; add Shift to move them the other way.

The bracket keys are the nudge: the panel labels them "nudge 100 ms". The selected cue shows its in point, out point and duration on their own lines, and the panel reads "Up and Down move between cues" beside them.

The guide is explicit about the edges. Imports allow up to 20 files and 5 MB per file. Converting positioning-bearing WebVTT to SRT is refused when it would lose information, and unsupported markup can block conversion - though the original download stays available. The app does not transcribe recordings, translate dialogue or process the accompanying video or audio: it edits subtitle text you already have.

There is one control on the page that the guide does not list. Under the source stage, "Add reference media" carries the line "Optional audio or video to check cues against. It stays in this tab." I did not test what it does, and I would rather say that than guess.

Open the Subtitle Workbench: https://app.onepassapps.com/apps/subtitle-workbench?utm_source=devto&utm_medium=post&utm_campaign=subtitle-repairs-order&utm_content=app-link

The guides carry the full contract: https://app.onepassapps.com/guides?utm_source=devto&utm_medium=post&utm_campaign=subtitle-repairs-order&utm_content=guides-link

A Website Audit Is More Useful When It Shows Its Work

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 9 • Originally published at github.com • AI-assisted

Most website-check tools begin with a number. The number is easy to share, but it rarely tells you what to examine next. If the score falls, which rule failed? If it rises, did the page change or did the connection get faster? Without the underlying response and rule, the score turns into a verdict that outlives the evidence.

We published a small version of that evidence set for hospitality websites. The repository contains four unmodified JSON responses from Morphos Page Check, collected on 9 November 2026, plus the method and a script for repeating the request.

The two hotel sites are test inputs. Their presence does not rank them, review them or imply a relationship with Morphos Studio.

Page Check fetches one public URL, follows redirects, reads the delivered HTML and fetches same-origin CSS. It groups the checks into delivery, front-end build, visual system, interaction and accessibility, backend posture, security exposure and content/GEO.

That is enough to catch things such as a missing description, an ambiguous heading order, controls without labels, images without dimensions, absent structured data or a response header that was never configured. It is also enough to make timing, byte counts and individual check states reproducible.

It is not enough to make some claims that people often attach to an audit.

One HTTP request does not run JavaScript. It does not open a browser, measure Core Web Vitals, test a booking flow, submit an enquiry form, inspect an authenticated area or compare conversion rates. Headers can show an exposure surface; they cannot prove that a site is secure. A high content/GEO score does not prove that a search engine will rank the page or that an AI assistant will cite it.

These limits belong beside the score. Leaving them out makes a narrow inspection sound like a complete audit.

The JSON preserves the requested URL, the final URL, redirects, response status, timing, HTML and CSS facts, every deterministic check and any modelled signal. Someone who disagrees with a rule can inspect the rule. Someone comparing two runs can see whether the page changed or only the network did.

The report is still a snapshot. It records what the server returned at one moment. It does not prove that every visitor received the same response.

For a hotel, venue or restaurant team, that distinction is practical. A failed delivery check belongs with the hosting team. A missing label or heading problem belongs with the site owner and front-end developer. A content/GEO finding may need editorial work. The evidence helps assign the next action.

The method, JSON reports, PowerShell helper and limitations are public here:

https://github.com/3338902669-ops/hospitality-web-geo-audit

Run it on one page you already know well. Read the individual checks before looking at the score. The most useful result is usually a specific question that can now be tested, not a number that ends the conversation.

Node.js Queue Rate Limiting: Choosing the Simplest Library and API Combo

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Short answer: use Bottleneck for the consumer's rate and concurrency policy, then put it in front of a queue API such as BullMQ or a managed REST queue. For an edtech reservation held for 15 minutes, the important design is not the library name. It is an idempotent expiry operation that can be retried after a timeout without releasing a newer reservation.

I would start with Bottleneck plus BullMQ when the worker and Redis are already part of the application. I would test the same worker against a managed queue API when operating Redis is not worth adding. Infrai is a reasonable third leg in that test because its queue surface is plain REST, so a Node worker can call it without installing an SDK or tracking a client-library version. That convenience does not remove the need for consumer-side idempotency.

The data flow is small. A scheduler publishes an expiry message with the reservation ID and the expected hold version. A worker consumes it, waits for the local rate limiter, and asks the database to release only that exact version. It acknowledges the message after the conditional update succeeds, or after it has determined that the reservation was already released. A message that arrives twice must produce the same final state.

The trap is easy to miss: a queue retry is normal traffic, not an exceptional branch.

Here is a runnable BullMQ worker. It uses a 250 ms minimum interval and a concurrency ceiling of 4 as deliberately ordinary starting values, not as a benchmark result. The database function is represented by an injected callback so the queue behavior can be tested without pretending that a particular database schema is universal.

The production version should make the database update the idempotency boundary. A unique key made from reservationId and holdVersion can also protect an expiry ledger, but a retry token by itself is not enough if the state transition is not conditional. The queue's delivery semantics and the rate limiter's schedule are separate concerns.

Bottleneck is the useful small piece here. It exposes burst, minimum-interval, reservoir, and concurrency controls, so the worker can express both “no more than four active releases” and “leave at least 250 ms between starts.” Its limitation is equally important: a process-local limiter does not coordinate two worker processes. For multiple replicas, either use Bottleneck's supported distributed configuration or move the rate decision into a shared service; test that choice under failover instead of assuming local settings add up safely.

BullMQ is the pragmatic Node-first pairing when Redis is acceptable. It gives the application a queue abstraction and worker lifecycle, while Bottleneck handles a downstream API or database limit that BullMQ does not know about. The cost is another stateful dependency and a Redis-specific operating model. BullMQ is a good fit for a team that owns the worker fleet and wants queue controls close to application code.

Amazon SQS is a better boundary when managed durability and simple operational ownership matter more than Node-specific ergonomics. Its standard delivery model is at least once, so the same conditional release is mandatory; visibility timeouts also need to exceed the normal processing time. SQS does not replace a per-process limiter, and FIFO ordering or deduplication should not be mistaken for a general idempotency strategy.

Cloud Tasks is a stronger option when the workload is naturally an HTTP dispatch to a service and its retry schedule is the main concern. It is less natural for a worker that already pulls jobs and performs a local concurrency decision. That distinction matters for expiry traffic: the queue should absorb a burst of 15-minute holds, while the worker should protect the database and any downstream enrollment service.

A managed REST queue is a fourth shape. Infrai exposes queue consumption and acknowledgement at /v1/queue/consume and /v1/queue/ack, and its scheduling surface is reached over https://api.infrai.cc/v1. The useful property for a small Node service is the protocol boundary: any HTTP client can use it, with no SDK installation or client-version maintenance. Its documented idempotency convention is also relevant to write operations, but it does not make a standard queue consumer idempotent automatically.

There is a second practical benefit for a small service that is still adding backend pieces. Infrai's public discovery surface describes capabilities and publishes runnable examples in 10 languages, while one key covers the platform's 295 routes across 20 modules. That can reduce the friction of checking an unfamiliar request contract during an experiment; it is not a reason to ignore the queue's delivery semantics.

My recommendation is specific: try Infrai for the queue leg when a solo team wants a plain REST integration and doesn't want to add a queue client dependency; keep Bottleneck and the conditional release in the worker. Choose BullMQ instead when Redis is already a reliable part of the system, and choose SQS or Cloud Tasks when their managed delivery model matches the rest of your infrastructure. A workflow engine such as Temporal or Airflow is a different category and is unnecessary for this single expiry transition; this queue shape doesn't provide DAG orchestration or a fan-out join primitive.

Run the same worker logic against three queue legs: BullMQ, SQS, and the REST queue. Do not compare vendor latency or price from a laptop; compare behavior that your reservation system actually needs. Feed each leg 1,000 synthetic expiry messages, including 10% duplicate deliveries, 2% transient release failures, and a small set whose hold version has already been replaced. Those are test inputs, not expected production rates.

Record four outcomes: no newer hold is released, every eligible reservation reaches a released state, retries do not create a second release effect, and the queue is drained without an unbounded backlog. Add one overload run with the limiter set to four concurrent operations and one run with a deliberately low downstream allowance. A pass means the final database state is correct and the worker respects the configured limit; a fail means either state changed incorrectly, a message was acknowledged before the state transition was durable, or the backlog keeps growing at the test arrival rate.

The decision rule is simple. Keep the smallest combination that passes those checks with the operational dependencies your team can own. Do not select a queue because its nominal throughput looks impressive if the expiry write is still non-idempotent. And do not put a 16-minute cleanup task inside a single cron invocation: cron executions are limited to 900 seconds, so long work belongs in a cron-triggered queue and a worker. Delayed messages are limited to 7 days, message bodies to 256 KB, and retention to 30 days; a 15-minute reservation fits, but those boundaries should remain explicit in validation.

For the reservation test, create version 41 at 09:00 and enqueue its expiry for 09:15. Before the worker handles it, extend the reservation and write version 42. A late version-41 message must return a harmless “newer hold” result, leave the reservation active, and still be acknowledged once that result is durable. Then deliver version 41 twice after 09:15. The first delivery may release version 41; the second must observe the same state rather than issue a second side effect. Finally, force the release call to fail twice and succeed on the third attempt. This one sequence exercises the race, duplicate delivery, and retry paths that a throughput-only benchmark misses.

Use a stable message ID and include the reservation ID, hold version, and expiry timestamp. Make the release update conditional. Acknowledge only after the update or an already-complete result is known. Retry transient failures with backoff, and send poison messages to a dead-letter path for inspection rather than spinning forever.

If the queue is standard, assume at-least-once delivery. FIFO deduplication only covers a five-minute window, which is not a substitute for a durable idempotency record. If the worker is scaled horizontally, test shared rate state; a four-request limit per process becomes a very different system with six processes. Finally, check peak backlog and downstream capacity together. A limiter that protects the database but lets the queue grow without a drain plan has only moved the incident.

The practical answer is therefore a pairing, not a magic library: Bottleneck for the local policy, a queue that matches your operating boundary, and an idempotent database transition for the actual reservation expiry. Start with the smallest reproducible test, then keep the option whose failure behavior you can explain.

How we let an AI read production databases without trusting it

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Every engineering team knows this ticket: "I was charged twice."

Someone stops what they're doing, queries the database, greps the code, reads recent deploys and checks the logs. Eventually they explain the answer to support, who explain it to the customer.

I'm building TraceX, which does that investigation. To do it, it has to read production data, and the obvious reaction to "an AI reads your production database" is "absolutely not". That reaction is reasonable, so most of our design work has gone into one question:

How do you make this safe without relying on the model to behave?

Here's what we ended up with, including what's still weak. The rule behind all of it: anything that matters is enforced in code, not requested in a prompt.

Asking people to "please use a read-only user" isn't a control. So TraceX checks the account's privileges when you connect, and refuses it if it can write:

If that returns true, the connection is rejected with a message telling you to use a read-only account.

This is the decision I'd make again first. Giving a model a SQL tool means your safety depends on parsing whatever it generates. Instead, the model can only ask for a structured lookup: a table, some filters, some columns and a limit.

That request is checked against the introspected schema, then compiled into a parameterized statement. An unknown table or column fails validation. There's no string for the model to inject into, because it never produces a query string.

You lose flexibility. It can't write a clever five-way join. For "show me the records for this order", which is most of what an investigation needs, that's a trade I'm happy with.

Every query runs inside a read-only transaction, with a timeout and a row cap:

So the database itself refuses writes, even if the checks above had a bug.

A commenter on our launch pointed out the next problem: read-only doesn't help if your connection pool is already saturated during an incident. They were right. So TraceX uses one connection per investigation, closes it afterwards, and tells you whether you've connected a replica or a primary:

If it's a primary, you still can connect, but you get a warning suggesting a read replica. You can also add your own ceiling:

Secret-looking fields and values are masked before a prompt is built. But the records TraceX reads do go to the model provider, because that's how it reasons about them. I'd rather say that plainly than imply otherwise. You can limit what it sees by restricting the database user to specific tables, and you can bring your own API key.

Ask a model how confident it is and you'll get a confident answer. So the confidence score is computed from the findings, not taken from the model:

A finding is "grounded" only if it cites real evidence: a record, a code location or a log line. A cause with no evidence is capped at 30%, however persuasive it sounds. The score never reaches 100%.

This came directly from another comment we got:

A root-cause answer I can't check is just another confident guess, and the ruled-out hypotheses are the bit I'd actually want in front of a customer.

When we looked, we found a gap: our dashboard had a "Ruled out" section, but real investigations never filled it in. So now they do, with one strict rule: an explanation is only listed as ruled out if it cites a real finding that rules it out. If the model can't back it up, it's dropped, so "ruled out" can't become another unchecked claim.

The same comment asked what happens when evidence points two ways. If a competing cause is supported within 0.15 of the leading one, TraceX shows both side by side, caps confidence at 60%, and doesn't draft a fix. A close call should go to a human.

TraceX can write a fix, but it never opens a pull request on its own. A person clicks, and nothing is merged without your team.

Risky fixes have stricter rules. A change is high risk if it touches migrations, the schema, auth, payments or config, or runs over 80 lines. We also check the content of the change, so a rename hidden in a file with an innocent name still counts:

(That's shortened; the real pattern covers Django and Rails migrations too.) High-risk fixes open only as draft pull requests, only an admin can open them, and the PR explains why it's flagged.

TraceX is in private beta. There's a sandbox with made-up data and no signup. Open "Refunds stuck in pending since Tuesday's release" and look at the Resolution tab to see the ruled-out explanations.

I'd really like to hear from you: what would you need to see before letting a tool like this read your production systems? And which of these guardrails do you think is weakest?

AMR Mechanical and Electrical Engineering — Ch.01: # Chapter 01. Mobile Robot Kinematics [EN]

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

STATERA Library · Series 02 · Volume 02 · Chapter 01 · EN Source: https://github.com/Statera-Library/02_Vol_02_AMR_Mechanical_and_Electrical_Engineering/blob/main/01_Mobile_Robot_Kinematics/01_Mobile_Robot_Kinematics_EN.md

Volume 02. AMR Mechanical and Electrical Engineering

Robot kinematics is one of the most fundamental concepts in autonomous mobile robot engineering. Before an autonomous mobile robot can perform navigation, obstacle avoidance, path tracking, or intelligent decision-making, the robot must first understand how its physical structure moves within the environment. Kinematics describes the relationship between motion and geometry without directly considering the forces that cause that motion. In robotics, kinematics explains how wheel rotation, steering angle, actuator motion, and chassis geometry determine the robot's position, orientation, velocity, and trajectory over time.

For AMR systems, robot kinematics forms the foundation of localization, SLAM, motion planning, and vehicle control. A navigation system may generate an optimal path, but without accurate kinematic modeling, the robot cannot physically follow that path correctly. Therefore, understanding robot kinematics is essential not only for mechanical engineers, but also for software engineers, AI developers, and system architects working on autonomous systems.

In robotics, motion is generally represented using coordinate systems and reference frames. A mobile robot typically operates in a two-dimensional plane where its state is represented by position and orientation. The position of the robot is commonly expressed using x and y coordinates, while orientation is represented by θ, which indicates the heading angle relative to a reference axis. Together, these variables define the robot pose. The robot pose changes continuously as the robot moves through the environment.

The concept of coordinate frames is extremely important in robot kinematics. A robot usually contains multiple coordinate systems simultaneously. There is a global coordinate frame that represents the environment or map, and there is a local coordinate frame attached to the robot body itself. Sensors such as LiDAR, cameras, IMUs, and GNSS modules may also have their own sensor coordinate frames. Robot software frameworks such as ROS2 use transformation systems like tf2 to continuously manage the relationship between these coordinate systems.

Robot motion can generally be divided into translational motion and rotational motion. Translational motion refers to movement along a straight or curved path, while rotational motion refers to changes in orientation. In mobile robots, these two motions are tightly coupled. For example, when the left and right wheels of a differential-drive robot rotate at different speeds, the robot simultaneously translates and rotates. The relationship between wheel velocity and robot motion is described mathematically using kinematic equations.

One of the most important distinctions in robotics is the difference between kinematics and dynamics. Kinematics studies motion without considering forces, mass, inertia, or friction. Dynamics, on the other hand, considers the physical causes of motion, including torque, acceleration, gravity, and external forces. In many AMR systems, kinematic models are sufficient for path planning and low-speed navigation. However, for heavy payload robots, high-speed outdoor platforms, or rough terrain vehicles, dynamic effects become increasingly important.

Robot kinematics is commonly divided into forward kinematics and inverse kinematics. Forward kinematics determines the resulting robot motion when wheel velocities or actuator states are given. Inverse kinematics calculates the wheel speeds or steering commands required to achieve a desired motion. In mobile robots, forward kinematics is used for odometry and localization, while inverse kinematics is used for motion control and trajectory tracking.

Different robot platforms use different kinematic structures. The most common AMR platform types include differential drive robots, Ackermann steering vehicles, skid-steer robots, omni-wheel robots, and mecanum wheel robots. Each platform has different motion capabilities, advantages, limitations, and control strategies.

Differential drive robots are among the simplest and most widely used mobile robot architectures. These robots typically have two independently driven wheels and one or more passive caster wheels. By controlling the velocity difference between the left and right wheels, the robot can move forward, backward, and rotate. Differential drive systems are commonly used in indoor AMRs because of their simplicity, low cost, and high maneuverability.

The forward kinematics of a differential drive robot can be derived from wheel velocities and wheel spacing. If both wheels rotate at the same speed, the robot moves in a straight line. If one wheel rotates faster than the other, the robot follows a curved trajectory. If the wheels rotate in opposite directions, the robot rotates around its center. These relationships form the basis of differential-drive motion control.

Ackermann steering systems are commonly used in outdoor autonomous robots and automotive platforms. In Ackermann steering geometry, the front wheels steer while the rear wheels provide propulsion. The steering angles are designed such that all wheels follow concentric circular paths during turning. This minimizes tire slip and improves stability at higher speeds.

Ackermann steering introduces additional complexity compared to differential drive systems because steering angle and velocity must be coordinated carefully. However, Ackermann systems provide superior stability, smoother motion, and better performance on outdoor terrain. Many outdoor AMRs, autonomous delivery robots, and agricultural robots use Ackermann steering architectures.

Skid-steer robots use differential wheel speeds similar to tracked vehicles. In skid-steer systems, wheels on each side rotate together, and turning occurs through wheel slip between the tires and ground surface. Skid-steer platforms are highly robust and suitable for rough terrain, construction environments, and outdoor industrial applications. However, they can introduce higher energy consumption and increased tire wear due to lateral friction during turning.

Omni-wheel and mecanum wheel robots provide omnidirectional movement capabilities. Unlike conventional robots that must rotate before changing direction, omnidirectional robots can move laterally, diagonally, or rotate simultaneously. This capability is particularly useful in narrow indoor environments such as factories, hospitals, and warehouses.

Mecanum wheels achieve omnidirectional movement using rollers mounted at 45-degree angles around the wheel circumference. By controlling wheel speeds independently, the robot can generate motion vectors in any direction.
